"For what it's worth," Xander said, after a moment, "I get a little of it. Not the same, not even close. But I've got all these instincts now that aren't mine either. Like I know how to land a punch I never learned how to throw. I know how fast I can run before it's too fast. Nobody taught me any of it. It's just there."

"That's not the same as thirty years and a dead husband, Xander."

"I know it's not," he said, rubbing the back of his neck. "I just mean I get a little of what it's like—having something in you that you didn't ask for and can't just set down because it'd be easier."

She considered that, something in her expression softening. "That's more than most people would think to say."

"So what do you do with it? The having-it part."

"I stay," she said, simply, like she'd only just said it out loud but had known it for days underneath. "Not because the tech back home doesn't fit anymore—and it doesn't, I'd feel it every day like a coat two sizes too small. Because this," she gestured, at the ship, at the ice outside the cracked viewport, "is closer to who I actually am now than a classroom full of teenagers ever could be again. I'm not Jenny Calendar anymore, not really. I'm not Beverly Crusher either, she never existed, not the way I did, not the way I still do. I'm something in between the two of them, something new, and the only place that makes sense for that is here, doing this work, with these people."

"What work? Fixing the ship?"

"That. Keeping the recovery teams alive. Teaching your people which parts of the Enterprise can help without killing anyone who tries to use them. Finding out whether Data can be restored. Making sure the technology on this ship doesn't become the cause of the very war it's supposed to prevent." She looked down at the Starfleet uniform she still wore. "And if any part of the crew exists somewhere, finding a way to learn that. I may never be able to. It remains my responsibility to try."

"That's a lot to choose."

"So was going home."

"Home's Buffy and Willow and Giles. School too, I guess. It's not just the house."

"No. Home rarely is."

"Maybe this is yours, then. Not because Beverly lived here. Because you're choosing it."

Jenny looked around the dark observation lounge. "The Enterprise was never Beverly's home by herself. It was the people. Without them, it's only a ship."

"There are people here now. Ferretti, Warren, all the recovery crews. Carter nearly moved into sickbay after one tour."

"She did look alarmingly comfortable."

"I'm just saying. Maybe home gets new people."

"Perhaps it does."

"Does anyone else know? What you just told me, I mean. The whole of it."

"No. And I don't think I'm going to tell them. Not the whole of it. Carter knows I'm carrying something. She doesn't know what, not really, not the specifics. Some things lose their shape the moment you try to put them into words."

"That's a hell of a thing to figure out by yourself."

"I'm not sure I have figured it out, not fully. I've just stopped pretending I haven't already chosen." A small, real smile, the first one he'd seen from her all evening. "You didn't come here to fix this for me, did you."

"No. I don't think I could if I tried. Wouldn't know where to start."

"Good. I didn't need fixing. I just needed someone who actually knew the truth of it to hear me say it out loud, all of it, without having to translate it into something smaller first." She picked the coffee back up and drank some, grimacing at the cold of it. "Thank you for that."

"Anytime," Xander said, and meant it. He stayed a while longer in the low light, neither of them saying much else, both of them looking out at the same endless white through the cracked glass.

---

The daily briefing began with the weather.

"The front passed south of the main site," Carter reported, one hand holding the radio handset and the other moving down her clipboard. "We had eleven hours of high winds and less accumulation than forecast. The exterior crews recovered all marked debris before visibility dropped. Two storage tents were damaged. No injuries."

"And the ship?" Hammond asked.

"The scaffolding held. The secondary hull shifted another four centimeters toward the original impact trench, then stopped. Warren's team added anchors this morning, but I don't want anyone in the exposed decks until the structural model is updated."

"Agreed. The international observers reached McMurdo this morning. Doctor Weir's office has confirmed one each from Russia, China, and Australia, plus a British engineering liaison. I want site rules in writing before they board any aircraft for the wreck."

O'Neill took the handset. "Already working on it, sir. Restricted sections stay restricted, everyone travels with an escort, and nobody puts a souvenir in their pocket unless they want to explain alien theft charges to five governments."

"Use more diplomatic language in the written version, Colonel."

"Carter's writing it."

"Then I have every confidence. What is the status of the altered section?"

Carter turned to a second page. "Deck Seven remains isolated from the ship's power grid. The optical monitor recorded one movement after shutdown, less than an inch of retraction in a single filament. Nothing since. That means the material retained some internal power after we cut the grid, or the shutdown response was delayed. We still can't call it inactive."

"Can it reach an occupied section?"

"Not without crossing two sealed bulkheads and an unpowered compartment. We've added independent motion and thermal sensors at both approaches. Commander Calendar is checking the computer logs for any attempt to reroute power."

"No foreign observer goes near that deck," Hammond said. "Doctor Weir will be informed that an active technological hazard is under containment. The existence of the Borg is not part of their briefing."

"That will raise questions," Carter said.

"Then we answer the questions we can without giving three governments a reason to demand samples of self-repairing hostile machinery."

"Yes, sir."

"Commander Data?"

"Stable, in the sense that his condition isn't changing. We've moved his components into a secured medical storage room with independent temperature control. I have sent all non-classified observations to Doctor Fraiser and the engineering group at the SGC. Nobody has identified a safe repair path."

"Understood. Continue documentation only."

O'Neill looked toward Jenny, who stood with Ferretti near the open hatch. She had declined to join the call herself, saying there was no point making a personal choice twice for two different audiences.

"Commander Calendar has decided to remain with the recovery operation," O'Neill said. "Indefinitely. She isn't putting a date on it."

"Her decision has been logged," Hammond replied. "Please extend my thanks for her continued cooperation, and let her know the offer of formal SGC support stands whenever she's ready to discuss what that might look like."

"Will do, sir."

Teal'c stepped closer to the radio with the latest sensor printout in one hand.

"The sensors are unchanged," Teal'c reported. "The signature beneath the ice has not fluctuated in the three days since it was first detected. Not by any measurable degree."

"Understood," Hammond's voice came back, tinny with distance. "For now, that's exactly where it stays. I've flagged it for the Joint Chiefs, but between the recovery operation and the situation with China, this is not the week to open a second investigation at that site. I trust you understand the reasoning, Teal'c, even if you don't care for it."

"I understand it. I do not entirely agree with it, but I understand it."

"Noted," Hammond said, dry, not unkind. "For what it's worth, neither do I, fully. But we don't have the resources to do this properly right now, and doing it improperly seems considerably worse than waiting."

"Yes sir," O'Neill said, next to Carter, arms crossed against the cold. "We'll keep an eye on it. Won't touch it, won't poke at it, just watch."

"Then that's everything for today. Hammond out."

The line clicked dead, static cutting to silence, and for a moment nobody said anything—then Ferretti turned back to his manifest, Casey and Sands hauled cable toward the scaffolding, grunting under the weight of it after hours of the same work, the low mechanical hum of the ship's emergency systems doing exactly what they'd been doing for three unbroken days.

Teal'c stood a while longer at the console, looking at the readout that hadn't changed since the moment he'd first found it. It did not spike. It did not fade. It simply persisted, steady, indifferent to the attention now being paid to it, to the sensors trained on it, to the questions being asked about it three thousand miles away in rooms he would never see.

Whatever waited down there was in no hurry.

He did not think that boded well for whoever eventually went to find it, and he said as much to no one at all, quiet, before switching off the console and going to help with the cable.

---

The observers arrived in an Enterprise shuttle because the weather had grounded the helicopters.

That fact did not improve anyone's mood.

Major Ferretti waited inside the main shuttle bay with O'Neill, Jenny, and two security officers while the craft settled onto the deck. Warren had flown it under Jenny's supervision, a decision made after McMurdo reported that the approaching wind would leave the delegation stranded for at least another day. The shuttle's side door opened, and four people stepped out wearing identical borrowed cold-weather gear over four very different sets of expectations.

"Welcome to the Enterprise," Jenny said. "I am Commander Calendar. Before we go any farther, I need to know that each of you received the site restrictions at McMurdo."

The British engineer, Commander Hale, held up a stapled packet. "Received and signed."

The Australian environmental officer did the same. The Russian observer nodded without producing his copy.

Doctor Jian Zhao, representing China, looked around the shuttle bay before answering. "I received a list of areas the United States has declared inaccessible. I did not agree that the United States possesses the authority to make that declaration."

"It doesn't," Jenny said.

Zhao turned back to her.

"I declared those areas inaccessible. This is my ship. The United States and United Kingdom are coordinating recovery with my permission. You are welcome here under the agreement Doctor Weir negotiated, but you are not authorized to enter a secured deck because your government disputes who typed the notice."

O'Neill kept his face neutral. Ferretti became suddenly interested in the shuttle's landing struts.

"Your status is itself disputed," Zhao said. "We have been told you crossed from another reality with no surviving government capable of recognizing your commission."

"True. I also know which damaged systems can flood a corridor with radiation before you reach the second door. If you prefer to base your safety on jurisdiction, Major Ferretti can take you back to McMurdo."

"The shuttle cannot safely make another flight," Warren said from the cockpit.

"Then Doctor Zhao can enjoy our guest quarters until the weather improves."

The Australian officer coughed into one glove, poorly concealing a laugh.

Zhao studied Jenny for several seconds. "I will observe the restrictions while reserving my government's position."

"That's all the agreement requires," Jenny said. "Major Ferretti will issue your badges. Red markings indicate escorted access only. Black indicates no access. If a door does not open, you do not force it. If you see black cabling, exposed machinery, green illumination, or anything moving without an obvious cause, you step away and call security."

"The technological hazard mentioned in our briefing," the Russian observer said. "What is its nature?"

"Self-repairing cybernetic material integrated into a damaged section of the ship," Jenny answered. "It is isolated and under observation. We do not yet know whether it is capable of spreading."

"You expect us to accept that description without inspection?" Zhao asked.

"I expect you to remain alive long enough to inspect the rest of the ship."

Commander Hale looked past them at the supports running along the shuttle-bay wall. "This compartment has shifted since the schematics we received were recorded."

"Four millimeters," Jenny said. "The hull settled during the storm. The occupied section is stable, but you'll wear a locator and remain with your escort."

The Australian officer opened her case and withdrew a compact sampling kit. "I need exterior ice samples from both impact trenches and beneath the debris staging area. Your report lists no detected release, but several compounds in the materials inventory have no terrestrial exposure standard."

"Carter will help you choose safe collection points," O'Neill said. "Nothing comes inside until Commander Calendar scans it."

"Agreed. I also want baseline water samples from the nearest downstream ice movement."

"Put it in the work plan," Ferretti said. "We'll get you transport when the wind drops."

The Russian observer had moved no farther than the edge of the shuttle's marked safety boundary. "And the artificial crewman? Commander Data."

Jenny's expression closed. "His remains are secured."

"Our cybernetics institutes may be able to assist."

"So may China's," Zhao said.

"Nobody examines him today," Jenny replied. "Captain Carter has completed a preliminary assessment. I will consider written proposals once I know who would perform the work, what they intend to do, and whether they understand that Commander Data is a person, not recovered equipment."

"A non-functioning machine may still be a person?" Zhao asked.

"Yes. If that prevents your institute from assisting, it saves both of us time."

Ferretti handed out the first badge. "We start with the saucer's forward compartments. Inventory teams work in pairs, one international observer and one recovery specialist. Nobody removes anything. If you find an object that isn't already tagged, mark the location and call it in."

The four observers accepted their badges. None looked satisfied. That, O'Neill thought, was probably the closest thing to proof that Weir's agreement was working.

As the group left the shuttle bay, Zhao slowed beside him.

"There are rumors of another discovery beneath the ice," she said quietly.

O'Neill did not look at her. "There are rumors of a lot of things."

"My government will ask formally."

"Then somebody considerably better dressed than me will answer formally. Stay with your escort, Doctor."

She moved on without another word.

O'Neill watched her join the others and wondered which satellite pass, intercepted report, or careless sentence had already made the buried structure a rumor. They had kept it secret for less than a week.

In his experience, most secrets collapsed under their own weight long before that.

---

"So that's it, then," Casey said, the next morning, watching Xander zip the satchel closed. "You're actually leaving."

"Gotta go be a normal person for a while. Turns out I've got homework."

"Homework." Sands shook his head. "Guy juggles a Humvee for fun and he's worried about homework."

"I'm not worried about it. I'm resigned to it. There's a difference."

Warren came over from the makeshift workbench where he'd been cataloguing pieces, wiping his hands on a rag that had clearly given up being useful several days ago. "Hey, before you go. Thanks. For the scaffolding, I mean. You probably saved us two weeks of work."

"Least I could do. You guys have been out here freezing your butts off for way longer than I have."

"Speak for yourself," Casey said. "Some of us have a healthy amount of insulation."

"Some of us also snore loud enough to register on the ship's sensors," Sands shot back.

"That was one time."

"It was every night, Casey, I have witnesses."

Ferretti appeared in the doorway, arms crossed, something almost fond in his expression. "You heading out, Harris?"

"Yes sir. Figured I'd say bye first, though. Seemed rude not to, after everything."

"Appreciate that." Ferretti held out a hand, and Xander shook it, careful, the way he'd learned to be careful with ordinary hands. "For what it's worth, kid, I've served with a lot of people. Most of them wouldn't spend their downtime hauling steel for a crew they just met."

"Figured I might as well be useful while I was here."

"You were. More than useful." Ferretti nodded toward the hull rising behind them. "Come back and visit sometime. I got a feeling this place isn't done being interesting."

"Yeah," Xander said, glancing at the ice, at the place where the readout had sat unchanged for days now, something he still didn't fully understand and hoped he wouldn't have to for a while yet. "Yeah, I get that feeling too."

After a last round of handshakes and one more bad joke from Sands, he walked past the scaffolding and the makeshift camp. Then he rose into the pale Antarctic sky, the wreck, the ice, and the patient mystery beneath it all shrinking away behind him.

---

The Enterprise repeated its deep scan after midnight.

The pulse passed through damaged hull plating, temporary scaffolding, wind-packed snow, and nearly two miles of ice. It touched the buried structure and returned to the ship in broken pieces, distorted by the same interference that had defeated every earlier attempt.

The computer compared the result against the previous scan.

Structure dimensions unchanged.

Energy field unchanged.

One life sign.

No measurable variation.

The result entered the sensor log. Carter's independent monitor copied it to the equipment bank outside Deck Seven. A communications program marked it for inclusion in the next report to the SGC. No alarm sounded because the system had been told that change was the danger, and nothing beneath the ice had changed.

Above, the recovery camp slept in shifts. The ship continued breathing recycled air through empty corridors. Snow moved over the debris field one grain at a time.

Far below all of it, the signal remained exactly where it had been.

---

The first tabletop exercise removed Xander from the board on purpose. Carter put a blank square where he would have been and asked the room to defend the Pacific without him.

The answer failed in eleven minutes. They reassigned the gaps to Enterprise sensors, SGC intelligence, Navy evacuation, and allied continuity. Xander’s name went on a separate line marked AVAILABLE CAPABILITY, never REQUIRED.

“We do not build around the strongest person we have,” Carter said. “We build one that survives when he is across the planet, injured, refusing, or simply not there.”

---



Chapter 19: Chapter 19



Sunnydale High smelled exactly the way he remembered, and somehow that was the strangest part of the whole morning.

Floor wax and old paper and whatever industrial-strength cleaner the janitors used on the bathrooms that never quite masked what it was covering up. A freshman knocked into his shoulder apologizing without looking up from his own feet, Snyder's voice crackled over the PA about gum, strictly prohibited as ever, and he was just Xander Harris again, late for first period, backpack half-zipped, same as every morning before Halloween.

"You're staring at your locker like it insulted you," Willow said, falling into step beside him, her own bag clutched to her chest.

"I forgot the combination."

"You've had that locker since freshman year, Xander."

I know! It's just... I feel like I'm watching a rerun, but I'm the only one who knows the ending. Everyone here is the same, but I'm not. And nobody can see the difference. It's like I'm a ghost in my own life." He tapped his temple.

"I can tell," Willow said, quieter, studying his face. "You look tired. Good tired. But tired. Like you've been carrying something heavy for a really long time and finally got to set some of it down, but not all of it."

"Guess sleeping in a former alien starship'll do that."

"Xander!" She glanced around at the crowded hallway, and he grinned despite himself.

Buffy caught up to them by the library steps, backpack slung over one shoulder. Her eyes did a quick, professional sweep—checking his posture, looking for the strain around his eyes, the way she'd started doing since their last patrol.

"So," she said. "First day back. How's it feel?"

"Honestly? Kind of amazing. Kind of terrifying. Mostly just really, really normal, which is its own kind of weird. Like the normal is the thing I have to get used to now, not the other stuff."

"Enjoy it while it lasts," Buffy said, sounding like she'd learned that lesson the hard way more than once, and steered them both toward class before the bell finished ringing.

Normal lasted until fifth period.

He was at his locker again, trading out books, half listening to Willow rattle off something about a chemistry assignment, when a shoulder caught him hard from behind, the exact same shove he'd taken a hundred times since freshman year, Kyle Bittaker putting his whole weight into it the way he always did.

Nothing happened.

Xander didn't move. Not an inch, not a stagger. Kyle, who'd built the whole shove around the expectation of Xander going into the lockers, went stumbling sideways instead, off balance, catching himself against the row with a clatter that drew more attention than the shove itself ever had.

"What the hell," Kyle muttered, staring at his own feet like they'd betrayed him.

Xander forced his shoulders to jerk sideways a half second too late, faked a stumble he didn't need, let his books scatter across the floor.

"Ow," he said, unconvincingly, bending to gather them while a couple of students exchanged a look he pretended not to notice.

"You okay?" Willow asked, low, once Kyle had wandered off, baffled and unsatisfied.

"Yeah. Just gotta remember to actually fall down sometimes. Apparently. Who knew that'd be the hard part."

"That's going to get harder before it gets easier," Willow said. "You know that, right? The pretending."

"Yeah. I know."

"You could just tell people, you know. Some people. Me, obviously, but I mean people like Kyle even, if it stopped him from trying to shove you into lockers."

"And then it's not a secret anymore. Then it's a thing everybody knows, and it changes how everybody looks at me, and I go from being invisible to being the guy who used to be invisible and now everyone's watching. I kind of like that Kyle has no idea. It's the last normal thing left."

"That's fair," Willow said, and let it go.

Down the hall, Principal Snyder stood half-hidden by his office door. His attention had nothing to do with hallway discipline; his small eyes tracked every second of the non-fight. When the hall emptied, he turned back inside without a word, his hand already reaching for the telephone.

An hour later Xander sat between Buffy and Willow at the end of a cafeteria table, eating pizza that had somehow managed to be both burned and cold. Willow complained about chemistry. Buffy stole the least burned piece from his tray and denied it while chewing. Somebody dropped a carton of milk three tables away and half the room applauded.

Nobody asked him to lift anything. Nobody offered him a job. Nobody called him Superman.

For twenty-three minutes, the most important decision in his life was whether the cafeteria cookie was worth the risk.

It was the kind of awful he wanted to feel again.

---

Giles asked him to stay in the library after the final bell.

Xander expected a lecture about the hallway, or a prophecy update from the Summers' kitchen. Instead, Giles locked the library doors, crossed to the table, and set his glasses down with a deliberate click, leaving them unpolished.

"Colonel O'Neill told me about the scars on your back," he said.

Every easy answer disappeared.

Xander stared at him. "He what?"

"He saw them during the medical testing in New York. He recognized what caused them."

"They're old."

"That is not a denial."

"It's not happening now."

"That is also not a denial."

Xander pushed his chair back, scraping it against the floor. "So what? It's over. He can't hurt me anymore, not unless he gets his hands on a tank. So it doesn't matter. It's handled.

"Your invulnerability does not make what he did acceptable."

"Didn't say it did. Just means it can't happen again."

"It means he cannot injure you in the same fashion. It does not make the house safe, and it does not require you to remain there."

"Where am I supposed to go?"

"You may stay with me."

The answer came so quickly that Xander had no joke prepared for it.

"Giles, your apartment has one bedroom."

"It also has a sofa, heat, and a notable absence of drunken men with belts. We can find something larger if the arrangement lasts. Buffy and her mother may be willing to help as well, though I have not told them why and will not without your permission."

"You already talked about this with O'Neill."

"We spoke about ensuring you had somewhere safe to go. We did not decide where you would live. That decision remains yours."

Xander looked down at the table. "If my parents find out the government offered money, they'll care where I live real fast."

"Then they need not find out. The White House is protecting your identity. As far as your parents are concerned, you have returned from an unusual school trip and are spending more time with friends. Given their previous level of attention, I doubt the change will be immediately challenged."

"And if it is?"

"Then I will speak to them. If that fails, Colonel O'Neill offered assistance that would be, and I quote, 'loud and obnoxious. Possibly violent.'"

A laugh escaped him before he could stop it. "He actually said that?

"With considerable menace."

"Sounds like him."

Giles put his glasses back on. "You do not have to protect your father from the consequences of his own behavior."

"I'm not."

"You are protecting everyone from the discomfort of knowing. That is not the same thing."

"What do you want, Giles? You want me to call the police and explain my dad used to hit Superman? You want Snyder hearing about it? Half the town?"

"No. I want you to pack what you need and come to my apartment tonight. Tomorrow, when there is time and no immediate danger, we decide what happens next. Quietly, if possible. Formally, if necessary."

"You make it sound easy."

"I assure you, I expect it to be extremely difficult."

"That's more honest."

"I am trying."

Xander pulled out a chair and lowered himself into it. "The worst part was the TV. Whenever he'd start, she'd just... turn the volume up. Like if it was loud enough, it wasn't happening."

Giles closed his eyes.

"Don't," Xander said. "Don't look like that."

"How should I look?"

"Like it isn't the end of the world. I've seen the end of the world. Or pictures of it, anyway. This isn't that."

"Pain need not be the end of the world to matter."

They sat in the library's after-school quiet. The only sounds were the clicking of old pipes in the walls and the rustle of a forgotten notice stirring in the air-conditioning vent.

"Your sofa's terrible," Xander said.

"I am aware."

"And you don't own a television."

"I own many books."

"Not helping your case."

"Will you come?"

Xander looked toward the doors, then back at the man who had offered without hesitation and still waited for an answer instead of deciding it for him.

"Yeah," he said. "For tonight. Then we figure it out."

"For tonight," Giles agreed.

It wasn't a solution, but it was a key in his hand. He wasn't sure he was ready to turn it, but for the first time, he knew the lock wasn't permanent. For now, that was enough.

---

Buffy found her mother's old address book on the kitchen counter that night, flipping through it without really meaning to while she waited for the kettle, and stopped on a page she hadn't looked at in months.

Her own name was in there, in her mother's handwriting, birthday circled, a note in the margin about her shoe size from two years ago that nobody had ever bothered crossing out. Ordinary. The kind of thing a normal girl's information looked like.

She thought about what Xander's own page would look like now, if anyone bothered to keep one. Powers, unknown limit. Weaknesses, unknown. Whereabouts, wherever the world happened to need him.

She traced the edge of the counter, thinking of Xander. He'd had his destiny thrown at him, same as her. She knew what that cost. But a small, mean part of her couldn't ignore the difference: when the world noticed him, it sent presidents and generals. For three years, her only backup had been a library full of books.

That could change. Giles had mentioned the people in uniforms, the ones who knew monsters were real. People who probably had files on 'hostile sub-terrestrials' and other things she just called Tuesday. They knew about demons, about Giles. Sooner or later, they'd connect the dots to Sunnydale. They'd come looking for the person who kept the graveyards quiet.

Buffy wasn't sure if that sounded like long-overdue backup, or just footsteps getting closer in the dark.

"You're allowed to feel weird about it," Giles said, from the doorway, and she startled, not having heard him come in.

"Feel weird about what?"

"Whatever it is you were just thinking, that had you frowning at your mother's address book like it had personally wronged you."

She closed the book, a little embarrassed. "It's not a big deal."

"I didn't say it was. I said you're allowed to feel it anyway." He came in, put the kettle on properly since she'd forgotten it was still just sitting there. "You've carried this town on your shoulders for three years, Buffy, largely without recognition, largely without thanks, and largely without choice. It would be rather strange if some part of you didn't notice the difference now."

"I don't want what he's got. The Oval Office, the whole world knowing his face. I really don't."

I don't imagine you do. But has anyone ever offered? Has anyone ever asked what you needed?"

She was quiet a moment, turning that over. "Is that awful? That I feel that even a little?"

"No," Giles said, simply. "It's human. You're allowed to be human about this, Buffy, even you." He found two mugs, set them out. "For what it's worth, I don't believe Xander would trade places with you for a moment, if he actually understood what your three years have cost. I think if you asked him honestly, he'd tell you he'd rather have what you have. Friends who chose to fight beside you, rather than a world that simply decided it needed you."

"Maybe," she said, and let it go, mostly, and made the tea.

---

Buffy peeled off after a vampire that came a little too close to the crowd outside the Bronze, sprinting into the dark, leaving Xander and Angel alone in the mouth of the alley, streetlight buzzing faintly overhead.

"You've been avoiding a question," Angel said.

"Have I?"

"Since the night you flew back into town. I asked what you were. You never actually answered, just deflected with a joke the way you always do."

Xander was quiet a moment, watching his own breath fog in the cold, and found, to his own surprise, that he wasn't inclined to deflect this time. "Kryptonian, I guess. Or whatever the Halloween spell decided that means, exactly. I don't fully understand it myself, if I'm being honest. I've got the powers, all of them, or most of them. I don't have the history, don't have the memories, don't have anything except what I figure out by trial and error."

"That's not a small thing to carry."

"No," Xander agreed. "It's not. Some days it feels like the only thing I'm carrying."

Angel looked at him for a long moment, something unreadable moving behind two hundred years of practiced stillness. "I've spent most of my existence not being human either. Not the way anyone standing near me is human. It doesn't stop mattering just because you get used to it, just because you learn to hide it well enough that people forget to ask."

"Does it get easier?"

"No," Angel said, honest in a way that cost him something. "You just get better at carrying it where people can't see it. That's not the same as easier. Don't mistake the two."

"Buffy know how you actually feel about all this? About what you are, day to day, underneath everything?"

"No. And I'd rather it stayed that way."

"Yeah," Xander said. "I get that more than I used to."

"Does Willow know? Buffy?"

They know I can lift a car. They don't know what it feels like to be the thing that can. Nobody gets that part." He shrugged, not looking at Angel. "Except you, maybe. Which is weird, since I'm pretty sure you'd still like to bite me most nights.

"Not tonight," Angel said, dry, and something in it was almost warmth.

They stood there a while longer, not friends, not quite adversaries either anymore, something quieter and more complicated settling between them instead.

"I still loathe you," Xander said, eventually, mostly to break the silence.

"I know," Angel said, and for just a second, something that might almost have been amusement crossed his face.

---

"Celia," the Mayor said, into the intercom, pressing the button with a single, precise finger, "any word from our friends at Wolfram and Hart?"

They're drawing up the terms now, sir. Mr. Manners said they should be ready for you within the week, but I can press him if you need it sooner.

"Good, good." He straightened the student photograph on his desk, corners worn now from handling. "And our young Mr. Harris?"

"Back in Sunnydale as of this morning, sir. Full time, according to the attendance records Principal Snyder forwarded."

The Mayor considered that, folding his hands, patient as ever, though something in the set of his shoulders had sharpened just slightly. "Well," he said, gaze fixed on the photograph. "That does change the timeline." He reached for the antibacterial gel, working it carefully between his fingers. "Celia, please inform Mr. Manners that we will require his draft by tomorrow. And clear my schedule for the afternoon."

---

Cordelia spotted him by the vending machines two days later, and the same nagging itch she'd been carrying since October flared up all over again.

"Harris," she said, sizing him up, arms already crossing. "You know you've got a real talent for looking exactly like a guy on the news I've been trying not to think about for days now."

"Small world," Xander said, feeding a dollar into the machine, not quite meeting her eyes.

"I'm serious. It's freaky. Same jaw, same everything, same stupid haircut even." She crossed her arms tighter. "You'd tell me, right? If you were secretly some kind of superhero. We're basically friends. Ish. Practically."

"Cordelia, if I were secretly Superman," Xander said, grabbing his soda, working to keep his voice light, "don't you think I'd have a cooler car by now? Because I'm still driving nothing, which seems like a real oversight on Superman's part."

That's a dodge, Harris, and a lame one. Try again.

"It's the only one you're getting."

"You know I'm going to figure it out eventually. I always figure things out eventually."

"Good luck with that."

She studied him a second longer, something in her expression not fully convinced, not fully willing to let it go, and then rolled her eyes and turned on her heel. "Whatever. Weirdo. Enjoy your gas station soda."

He watched her walk away, and only when she turned the corner did he realize his jaw had been clenched the whole time. She never did look fully satisfied. He filed that away next to a dozen other small worries, and didn't know yet what he was going to do about it.

---

Ethan was waiting for him on Giles's fire escape, a bottle and two glasses set between them on the metal grating. The city spread out below, a scatter of ordinary lights. Ethan poured a measure into one glass, leaving the other empty.

"Sit," Ethan said, and Xander did, settling onto the cold metal steps beside him.

"This a social visit?"

"Not remotely." Ethan poured himself a measure and didn't offer Xander any. "I need you to understand something, and I need you to actually hear it, not simply nod along the way you're rather good at doing when you'd prefer not to think too hard about a thing."

"Okay."

There's a clock ticking. For you, for me, for this whole town. We don't have the luxury of failing, because there is no 'after' if we do. Understand?

"So what do we do?"

"We begin properly." Ethan set his glass down with a small, deliberate click. "I'm not a soldier, whatever you might have gathered from your own future. I can't teach you to throw a punch better than the Colonel already can, and I wouldn't insult either of you by trying. What I can teach you is how to feel magic coming before it arrives, how to ward against it, how to recognize when a spell aimed at you feels wrong before it lands, before it's too late to matter."

"Show me. Now, I mean. Something small."

Ethan considered him a moment, then held out his hand, palm up, a small, flickering light kindling above it, blue-white and cold. "Close your eyes. Tell me when you feel it change."

Xander closed his eyes. For a long moment, there was only the cold. Then a warmth bloomed against his eyelids, faint but distinct, like standing in a patch of sun.

"Now," he said.

"Good," Ethan said, and the light guttered out. "That was benign. Warmth, mostly harmless. Something aimed to actually harm you will feel different. Sharper. It won't be warmth on your skin, it'll be a needle pressing against it. You'll learn to feel the point before it breaks through. You'll learn the difference faster than you think. Everyone does, once their life depends on it."

"That's not exactly reassuring."

"No. It wasn't meant to be."

Ethan reached into his coat and produced a piece of white chalk, a length of red thread, and an iron nail darkened with age. He placed all three on the metal step between them.

"First lesson," he said. "Magic is not a color of light. It is not heat, electricity, or any other tidy force your Captain Carter can measure with a machine. The light was theater. What you felt was intention attached to it."

"Your intention?"

"A very small piece of it. Intention gives magic direction. Form gives it rules. Power makes it happen. Confuse those three and you'll mistake a bright display for a dangerous spell while the quiet one behind you takes your head off."

"Still doing great on the reassuring part."

"I contain multitudes."

Ethan drew a circle no wider than his palm on the step, then bisected it with three short lines. He placed the nail across the center and tied the red thread around it with a knot too complicated for Xander to follow at full speed.

"Watch again," Ethan said.

He untied the thread and repeated the knot slowly. Over, beneath, twice around the nail, then through a loop that appeared where Xander had not expected one.

"What does it do?"

"At the moment, nothing. Chalk is chalk. Thread is thread. Iron is useful because the supernatural world has spent several thousand years agreeing that it ought to be, but agreement alone doesn't make a ward."

He pressed two fingers to the chalk mark and spoke three words Xander did not recognize. The air above the step tightened. Nothing lit up. There was no sound. Xander felt pressure against his skin, faint but unmistakable, as if he had walked into a room where an argument stopped the instant he entered.

"Now touch the nail," Ethan said.

Xander reached for it. His fingers stopped half an inch above the iron.

There was no physical barrier. He knew that as certainly as he knew he could push his hand through the metal stairs if he wanted. Every instinct he had told him not to close the remaining distance.

"That's creepy."

"Good. Creepy keeps one attentive. Push through it. Slowly."

"You just said it was a ward."

"Against casual contact, not force. A locked door and a fortress are both barriers. Learning which stands before you is part of staying alive."

Xander lowered his hand. The pressure sharpened, climbed his wrist, and became a cold ache behind his eyes. He stopped.

"Could I break it?"

"Easily."

"Should I?"

"No."

Xander pulled his hand back. The discomfort vanished immediately.

"Second lesson," Ethan said. "Being capable of breaking a thing is not evidence that breaking it is wise."

"O'Neill's been teaching that one too."

"Then for once the Colonel and I are in complete agreement. A landmark occasion."

Ethan scuffed through one of the chalk lines with his thumb. The pressure collapsed.

"The ward had four anchors," he explained. "The circle defines the boundary. Iron gives the boundary weight. The knot holds the working after I stop feeding it power. The spoken phrase tells it what to resist. Break any one and the ward fails."

"So I look for the chalk."

You look for the anchors. Competent witches don't use chalk. They'll carve a symbol into the mortar of a brick, or tie the ward to a name in a book, or even a memory. Anything that lasts.

"A person?"

"Blood is traditional. Pain is cheap. Love is stronger than either and considerably harder to arrange."

Xander stared at him. "People use love to power spells?"

"People use everything to power spells. Magic is not made moral by the fuel."

Ethan handed him the chalk. "Draw it."

Xander copied the circle and lines, then wrapped the thread around the nail. His first knot came apart. The second held but left the loop on the wrong side. Ethan made him undo it without comment.

On the fourth attempt, the shape matched.

"Now the words," Ethan said, and taught them one syllable at a time.

Xander spoke the phrase. For a moment nothing happened. Then the air trembled and the chalk circle pushed against his awareness, weaker than Ethan's but present.

"I did it."

"You assembled a ward after copying every movement from a man who supplied the power and prepared materials. Try to contain your arrogance."

"You could just say good job."

I could. You'd spend half the time unlearning what I taught you."

But Ethan was smiling when he broke Xander's circle.

"And the other thing? The stuff you actually lived through?"

Ethan was quiet a moment, staring at the space where the light had been. "In the years I remember, I saw two kinds of people die. The ones who rushed in because they couldn't stand the waiting, and the ones who hesitated because they couldn't accept what they'd become. The war got them both. It hardens you, or it breaks you. I'd rather you learned that lesson from me than from it.

"Give me one. A mistake, I mean."

Ethan turned the silver ring on his finger once. "There was a shelter outside Carson City during the first winter. An old mine with one entrance, easily defended. We placed a ward over it that would stop a Goa'uld host, a Jaffa, or anything carrying a symbiote. Forty-three people lived behind it."

"What happened?"

"The ward worked. That was the problem. Everyone began treating it as if it could do more than we had told it to do. They stopped posting a proper watch. A group of human collaborators walked through carrying conventional explosives and collapsed the entrance."

Xander looked at the broken chalk circle between them.

"Forty-three?"

"Thirty-eight dead before we opened another route. Five survived. The people who built the ward blamed themselves because grief prefers a person to a principle. But the ward had done precisely what it was made to do. We failed because we began believing protection against one danger meant safety from every danger."

"So what's the lesson? Never trust magic?"

"Never ask a defense to make decisions for you. Know what it stops. Know what it permits. Keep watch anyway. That applies to magic, military plans, secret identities, and your new skin."

Xander rubbed one thumb across his palm. "You think I'm doing that. Treating this like it makes me safe."

"I think you could. I think everyone around you could. Invulnerability is a very seductive word, especially to frightened people."

"And you?"

"I know better. I helped bury the ones who believed it."

"How bad does it get? For me, I mean. In the version you remember."

Ethan didn't answer right away, turning the glass in his hand. "Worse than I'm willing to tell you tonight. Ask me again closer to March. There's an answer that will help you prepare, and there's one that will just scare you. I'm going to give you the first one, but only when it's time."

"That's not comforting."

"It wasn't meant to be comforting. It was meant to be honest." He looked out over the city a while. "We have time yet. Not as much as either of us would like, not nearly as much as I'd choose if I were given the choosing of it. But some. Enough, perhaps, if we don't waste it."

He turned the plain silver band on his finger, a gesture Xander was beginning to recognize. Some instinct told him not to ask about it, not tonight.

They remained there a while longer in the cold, the city quiet below them, until one of them finally exhaled and neither moved to leave.



Chapter 20: Chapter 20



The watch vibrated once against the inside of Xander's wrist during second-period homeroom.

One long pulse. Call.

He kept his eyes on the worksheet in front of him and wrote the answer to question seven as if a military satellite had not just reached into Sunnydale High to tap him on the arm.

Across the aisle, Willow looked up.

Xander finished the sentence, set down his pencil, and glanced at the clock above the chalkboard. Nine-fifteen exactly. Carter had said nine-fifteen, plus or minus thirty seconds. Right on schedule.

Willow raised her eyebrows.

He moved his wrist just enough for her to see the black digital face and gave the smallest possible nod.

At the front of the room, Mrs. Moran was trying to persuade thirty teenagers that writing three goals for the semester would improve their chances of achieving any of them. Xander had written pass chemistry, figure out where he was sleeping next week, and do not get a country invaded. The list felt less like a joke every day.

The bell rang four minutes later.

Willow was beside him before he reached the door. "Was that it?"

"Scheduled test. One long. Call when practical."

"And practical is now?"

"Practical is before Snyder decides hallway movement has become a privilege available only through written application."

Buffy fell in on his other side. She had heard enough to look at the watch rather than ask what they were discussing.

"It looks normal," she said.

"That's because Carter understands secret identities and also fashion accessories sold at the mall."

"Does it do calculator things?"

"No."

"Stopwatch?"

"Yes."

"Alarm?"

"Yes."

"Global crisis hotline?"

"Apparently."

"So it's less useful than a calculator watch but more likely to ruin lunch."

"That's pretty much the sales brochure."

They turned toward the library. Xander had the designated number memorized, but the administration kept the nearest student-accessible telephone in the main office, where a call lasting more than forty seconds would require an explanation. Giles's office had a private line and a door.

The library was empty except for Giles, who looked up from a stack of returned books as the three of them came in.

"The test?"

"Right on time."

Giles unlocked his office without comment. Xander dialed the long-distance number Carter had written on a card and made him burn after memorizing it. The line clicked twice before a woman answered without identifying any organization.

"Authorization."

Xander gave the six-digit code paired to the receiver.

"Hold."

There was no music. Willow leaned into the doorway. Buffy leaned against the opposite frame, watching the watch.

A man's voice came on. "Receiver test one. Identify the signal received."

"One long. Standard call request."

"Correct. Remain on the line for urgency test."

The watch gave one long pulse.

Three seconds later, it gave another.

Then a third.

"One long repeated three times," Xander said. "Urgent call. Leave what I'm doing and contact you immediately if I can do it safely."

"Correct. The test is complete. No response or travel is requested."

"That's it?"

"That's it. Thank you."

The line went dead.

Xander replaced the receiver. "Mountain people have really mastered the warm goodbye."

"Can you ignore it?" Buffy asked.

Buffy’s question hung in the air. Willow would have started with the technical specs—satellite footprint, signal interference, encryption protocols. But Buffy went straight to the heart of it: who was in charge? She wanted to know if the thing on his wrist was a tool, or a leash.

"Yeah. Off switch is underneath. They can't tell if I got anything, and they can't tell if I turned it off."

"What about the urgent one?"

"Still a request. Just one where waiting for the bell is supposed to be a genuinely bad idea."

Buffy considered that. "Who decides genuinely?"

"Duty officer has criteria. I have judgment. If they disagree, mine wins while I'm wearing it."

"Good."

Giles's expression eased by a degree.

"And the Superman one?" Willow asked.

Xander lowered his voice, though the library remained empty. "Flat receiver clipped inside the uniform. Same patterns, same address, separate off switch. I don't wear the same watch in both identities and hand Cordelia a photo comparison that ruins my life."

"Cordelia does have a remarkable eye for accessories," Giles said.

They all looked at him.

"I have supervised her in a library for more than a year. One learns things against one's will."

The warning bell sounded.

Xander pulled his sleeve over the watch. "Back to the part of my day where the worst thing that can happen is failing a chemistry quiz."

They stepped into the hall and nearly walked into Snyder.

He stood ten feet away, motionless amid the current of students, his attention fixed on Xander's covered wrist.

"Mr. Harris."

"Principal Snyder. Great morning for education."

"Why were you in the library?"

"Book emergency."

Snyder looked past him at Giles. "During passing period."

"The books get restless if you don't check on them."

"Get to class."

Xander went. He could feel Snyder watching until the crowd carried him around the corner.

---

The real signal came during sixth-period history.

Mr. Whitmore was drawing the Missouri Compromise on the board when the watch pressed one long vibration against Xander's wrist.

He looked down.

Three seconds.

Another long pulse.

A cold dread washed through him, tighter than any classroom anxiety. 

Urgent call. 

The morning’s test had been a game, a secret kept under a worksheet. This wasn't a game.

Xander raised his hand.

"Mr. Harris?"

"I need to go to the nurse."

"Can it wait twelve minutes?"

The third pulse ended.

"No. Sorry."

He was already gathering his books.

"Mr. Harris, I did not excuse you."

"I know."

He left anyway.

Half the class turned to watch. Mr. Whitmore called his name once more, sharper, but did not follow.

Xander walked until the door closed, then ran.

The library was occupied by three freshmen doing research at the tables. Giles was behind the counter. He saw Xander's face and crossed to the office before Xander needed to say anything.

"Urgent call," Xander said.

Giles shut the door behind him. Xander dialed.

"Authorization."

He gave the code.

This time the hold lasted less than two seconds.

“Superman response desk. We have an authorized international assistance request. Southern Brazil…”

The voice was calm, professional, and laid out the facts like a weather report: wildfire, trapped vehicles, forty-three people in immediate danger. The details blurred into a single, sharp point. Are you available?

The words came quickly, each one arranged to be answered, not obeyed.

"How long until the fire reaches them?"

"Local estimate is fourteen minutes before the eastern front crosses the trapped section. Conditions are changing."

"Coordinates?"

The officer read them. Xander repeated the numbers while Giles wrote them on a scrap of catalog paper.

"I'll go."

"Acknowledged. Incident commander is Captain Ana Ribeiro, Rio Grande do Sul fire service. She speaks English. Report to the command position south of the highway before entering the evacuation corridor. Do not use heat-producing abilities without her approval. Fuel tankers are operating in the area."

"Wasn't planning to."

"Call this number after release from local command."

The line disconnected.

Giles was already moving toward the locked cabinet behind his desk. The Superman uniform lay inside a plain canvas bag beneath three boxes of accession forms. Xander had complained about bringing it to school until Giles asked whether he preferred flying home in street clothes while news helicopters followed.

"Fourteen minutes," Xander said.

"Then I suggest you change."

The office door locked. Xander pulled the recessed switch beneath the watch face with the tip of a paper clip, watched the display go blank, and stripped off his shirt.

The uniform felt the same, fitting like it had always been his. The world, however, warped around it. In jeans and flannel, he was a kid ditching class, facing detention. In blue and red, he was a number in an equation, the variable that might stop forty-three people from burning to death on another continent.

He clipped the flat receiver inside the uniform at his waist and switched it on.

"Tell Buffy?" he asked.

"I will."

"And Whitmore?"

"I will attempt to produce an explanation that does not involve Brazil."

"Tell him I threw up. Nobody asks follow-up questions."

Xander opened the office window.

"Xander."

He looked back.

"Report to the incident commander first."

"I heard them."

"I know. Hear me as well."

Xander nodded and climbed through the window.

He kept below the roofline until the school buildings blocked the courtyard, then rose.

Sunnydale dropped beneath him, then the grid of streets dissolved into the brown sprawl of the valley, then the valley compressed into a crease between mountains, and the world narrowed to numbers Giles had written on catalog paper.

It lay across the southern horizon in a brown-black sheet, wide enough to make the problem look less like a fire than a change in the sky. Xander slowed hard as the ground came into focus. Roads. Fields. A line of red vehicles beside a cleared pasture. People moving around a temporary command post beneath two yellow canopies.

He landed outside the activity rather than in the middle of it.

Every head turned.

A woman in soot-streaked yellow gear came toward him. She was in her forties, compact, hair braided beneath her helmet. A radio snapped rapid Portuguese at her shoulder.

"Superman?"

"Captain Ribeiro?"

"Yes. Come."

She did not stare after that. She took him to a map weighted at the corners by water bottles and pointed.

"Fire crossed here twenty minutes ago. Convoy turned back. This truck rolled when the driver avoided a tree. It blocks both lanes. Coach here, coach here, ambulance between. Fire engine at the rear lost steering. We have crews trying from the west, but the wind shifted."

The radio at her shoulder spoke again. Ribeiro answered, listened, then pointed to a red line drawn across the highway.

"Eastern fire front is eight hundred meters. Maybe less. Can you move the truck?"

"How much fuel?"

"Diesel. Tank damaged, leaking. No fire yet."

"I can move it. Where do you want it?"

She looked at him for the first time as if recalculating. "Off the road, north side, thirty meters past the drainage cut. Keep it upright."

"People first or truck first?"

"Truck. My crews cannot reach people until road is open. Then the front coach. Its rear axle is caught under timber. Do not move the ambulance unless the medic tells you. One patient has spinal injury."

"Got it."

"You report when road is open. I decide what moves next."

"Got it," Xander repeated.

He launched.

The heat rose before the trapped convoy appeared. Trees burned on both sides of the road, not like campfires but like structures coming apart, branches throwing orange across the smoke. The highway vanished beneath a lattice of fallen trunks. Beyond them, a white fuel truck lay across both lanes, cab against the guardrail, rear wheels suspended over a ditch.

People were crowded inside the first coach. Faces pressed to glass.

Xander landed beside the truck. Diesel spread in a dark stream beneath it. He could hear metal contracting in the heat and, from inside the buses, the separate, crowded thud of dozens of pulses.

He crouched, put both hands beneath the tank where the frame would carry the load, and lifted.

The truck came up too easily. That remained the most dangerous part. A thing that weighed twenty tons felt no more informative in his hands than a chair. He had to watch the frame, watch the damaged tank, watch the driver's cab so it did not swing through the guardrail.

He rose three feet, moved north, and set the truck upright beyond the drainage cut.

The torn line kept leaking.

Xander tore a strip from the truck's rubber mudflap, folded it over the puncture, and crimped the surrounding metal against it with his fingers. The flow slowed to a drip.

He flew back to the command post.

"Roadblock moved. Fuel leak pinched off, not repaired."

Ribeiro relayed it. "Coach axle next. There are two firefighters at the engine. One has a broken leg. Front is now five hundred meters."

"Which first?"

She looked at the map, then at the smoke. "Firefighters. Engine fuel tank is protected but not forever. Bring both here. Then coach."

Xander went.

The fire engine stood at an angle against a burning embankment. One firefighter lay behind its front wheel while the other tried to drag him by the shoulders. Flames moved through dry grass toward the underside of the engine.

Xander landed between them and the fire.

"Captain Ribeiro sent me. I'm moving you both."

The standing firefighter stared once, then nodded and shouted something to the injured man.

Xander lifted them together, one on each arm. The injured firefighter screamed when his leg shifted. Xander froze.

"Sorry. Sorry."

"Go," the other man said in English. "Go now."

Xander held the broken leg against his side as carefully as he could and rose above the smoke.

At the command post, medics took them. Ribeiro pointed back before he landed fully.

"Coach."

The eastern fire front had become visible through the trees.

Xander cleared the timber from around the front coach in armfuls, throwing each burning trunk into an already blackened field where it could not start another line. The rear axle had bent around a fallen eucalyptus. He tore the trunk apart lengthwise rather than lifting the bus onto thirty frightened passengers.

The driver leaned out his window. "Can move!"

"Captain says go west! Stay behind the cleared lane!"

The coach started. Its damaged rear wheel screamed against the road but turned.

The second coach followed. The ambulance remained.

Xander flew back.

"Two coaches moving west. Ambulance still in place."

Ribeiro was listening to three radios now. "Ambulance cannot move. Fallen power pole ahead. Live line across road."

"I can move the pole."

"Line may be energized."

"That part's okay. Where?"

She gave him the location. "After line is clear, ambulance follows. Fire engine stays. We abandon it."

"I can move the engine too."

"If there is time."

There was time because he made it.

He lifted the power line by its wooden pole and carried the whole assembly away from the road, sparks snapping across his arms without sensation. He cleared the ambulance, then returned for the disabled engine after the last vehicle had begun moving. Fire rolled over the embankment as he lifted it. Paint blistered beneath his hands. He carried it above the trees and set it in the pasture beside the command post.

For a few seconds, everybody watched the fire engine settle onto the grass.

Then Ribeiro's crews went back to work.

The evacuees were transferred to waiting vehicles. The injured firefighter disappeared inside an ambulance. Radios crackled in a language he didn't understand. The machine of the disaster response ground on without him. He was a tool that had been used, and now it felt like he was being set aside.

Ribeiro came over after six minutes.

"The corridor is empty. Our crews are withdrawing to the southern break."

"Do you need me there?"

"Can you carry water?"

"A lot. But I don't know where you want it, and dropping it from high up might hurt people."

"Correct." She looked toward the smoke. "We have aircraft arriving. Wind is changing again. Your work here is complete."

The dismissal surprised him.

"That's it?"

"Thirty-seven civilians, six responders, two coaches, one ambulance, two engines, and a fuel truck are no longer inside my fire. That is not nothing."

"I could stay."

"You could. You do not know this fire, this terrain, my crews or our plan. Extraordinary strength does not make you incident commander."

There was no insult in it. Ribeiro said it as a fact she expected him to understand.

"Right."

She held out a gloved hand. "Thank you."

Xander shook it carefully.

"If your people ask," she added, "tell them the request was answered and you were released by local command at seventeen forty-three."

"They'll ask."

"Good."

He rose above the field.

The fire remained enormous. From the air, the corridor he had cleared was a thread at its edge. Aircraft approached from the south, orange and white against the smoke. Fire crews repositioned below according to a plan that had existed before he arrived and would continue after he left.

He had not saved Brazil. He had moved a handful of people out of the fire's path. It felt small against the smoke-choked horizon.

But it was the job they had called him for. And for today, that had to be enough.

---

The clock in Giles's office said 3:38 when Xander climbed through the window.

He stared at it for several seconds before remembering time zones.

His history class had ended twenty-three minutes ago.

The uniform smelled of smoke. Ash had worked into the seams at his shoulders and left a gray smear across the red on his chest. He switched off the concealed receiver, changed, and turned the watch back on.

Giles waited outside the office. Buffy and Willow were at the table behind him.

"Forty-three people," Xander said before anyone could ask. "All out. Local command released me. Nobody died while I was there. One firefighter had a broken leg."

Giles nodded once. "And you?"

"Smoke doesn't really bother me. Fire didn't either. I got sparks on me from a power line. Also fine."

Buffy came closer and pinched the sleeve of his flannel shirt.

"You smell like a barbecue."

"International rescue barbecue."

"Did you put out the fire?" Willow asked.

"No. I moved the people and the vehicles they couldn't reach. Captain Ribeiro said I didn't know enough to help with the rest."

Buffy smiled slightly. "I like her."

"You'd get along. She orders people around and gets annoyed when they think being strong makes them qualified."

"I really like her."

“Mr. Whitmore reported you for leaving class,” Giles said.

The words took a second to register. Brazil. The fire. The weight of the truck in his hands. It all dissolved, leaving him standing in a quiet library, smelling of ash, about to get detention. The world snapped back into place.

"Right. School."

"Snyder has requested you appear in his office tomorrow morning."

"I rescued a bus. Two buses."

"I doubt he will consider jurisdictional distance a defense."

Xander looked at the history book still lying where he had dropped it. "I went from the Missouri Compromise to Brazil and back before the last bell."

"Still have chemistry homework," Willow said gently.

"That seems personally insulting."

Buffy studied him. "You okay?"

"Yeah. I mean, yes. People were going to burn, and then they didn't." He rubbed soot between his fingers. "It was just a school day. Then it wasn't. Then it was again."

"That part doesn't get normal," Buffy said.

She sounded certain.

Xander looked at her. He saw the familiar exhaustion in her eyes, the set of her shoulders that was older than any teenager's. She had lived this weird, fractured life for years, fighting monsters in the dark and then sitting through algebra class like nothing had happened.

"No," he said. "I guess it doesn't."

---

Giles's apartment was quiet. Too quiet. Xander had showered until the water ran clear, but he could still smell smoke in the fabric of his clean shirt. He stared at his chemistry homework, the question about ionic bonds looking like a language from another planet.

He couldn't focus. He kept seeing faces pressed against a bus window. 

He pulled an old grocery receipt from his wallet. Zoey's handwriting felt impossibly normal next to the routing phrases and verification codes for the White House switchboard. It had sounded so simple in Washington. Now it felt like crossing another world.

They sounded less straightforward when an operator answered, "White House."

"Hi. I need to speak to Zoey Bartlet."

Silence.

"May I ask who's calling?"

"Xander Harris. She gave me a routing thing. Georgetown Blue, then seven-one-four."

"One moment."

The line clicked.

Another operator asked his name, then his location, then the routing phrase again. Xander provided all three.

"Please hold."

Music came on this time. Instrumental, patriotic, and faint enough to make him wonder whether the White House had chosen it or whether every secure telephone system came with government-approved trumpets.

Four minutes passed.

The music stopped.

"Hello?"

Zoey sounded breathless.

"Hey."

"Xander?"

"Unless the switchboard rerouted me to another teenage boy who knows your private code."

"Give them a week. How are you calling me through the White House?"

"You told me to."

"I gave you the number because I thought you might call. I didn't think you would use it correctly on the first try."

"Your confidence is touching."

"Where are you?"

"Giles's sofa."

"Is that in California?"

"Most days."

"Most days?"

Xander looked down at the chemistry book. "I left history class for Brazil."

There was a pause.

"You left history class for Brazil."

"There was a fire."

"Of course there was."

"Two buses, an ambulance, firefighters. The watch did the urgent thing."

"The watch works?"

"Very works. Satellite reaches Sunnydale. Sunnydale reaches my wrist. My wrist ruins sixth period."

"Are the people okay?"

"Yeah. Broken leg on one firefighter. Everybody got out. The fire is still going, but people who actually know fires are doing that part."

"And you came back to school?"

"Technically school ended while I was over the Pacific. I came back to chemistry homework."

Zoey laughed. It was not a polite laugh or the careful one she used in rooms where people watched her father. Xander leaned back against the sofa and let the sound reach him across the country.

"How was your day?" he asked.

"Comparatively embarrassing."

"Try me."

"My detail followed me into the campus bookstore because they decided the upper floor had insufficient visibility. Then one of them stood in American Literature while I bought shampoo."

"Maybe he likes American literature."

"He was facing the shampoo aisle."

"Maybe he likes American shampoo."

"Then I got escorted to a seminar on democratic participation by three armed men who checked the room first."

"That feels participatory."

"I know why they're there. I know what happened the last time I slipped them. I know I was wrong. That doesn't make it less weird when somebody inspects the bathroom before I use it."

"I know."

The answer came out quieter than he intended.

Zoey went quiet too.

"Do you?"

Not the bathroom part. Pretty sure our school bathrooms would win that fight. But… yeah. The rest of it. It’s like, one thing happens to you, and suddenly your life has all these new rules. And the worst part is, they mostly make sense. So you can’t even get properly mad about it without feeling like a jerk.

"Exactly."

"I got a satellite watch."

"You said."

"I got two satellite watches, actually. One's not a watch. Secret identity thing. There are protocols and phone numbers and people on duty who can call me from anywhere on Earth. I left class, flew to another continent, and a fire captain told me where to put a fuel truck."

"That sounds organized."

"It is. That's the thing. It's really organized."

He picked at a scorch mark on the cuff of his flannel. It came away beneath his thumbnail.

"For me," he said. "After, what, eleven days? Two weeks? Everybody knows where I'm supposed to call. There are military satellites and international permissions and a person whose job is deciding whether something counts as urgent."

"You don't sound happy about that."

"I am. I think. Those people would've died if nobody could reach me. Captain Ribeiro knew what she was doing, and the guy on the phone gave me facts instead of ordering me around. That's all good."

"But?"

"But I came back and Buffy was in the library."

The sentence sounded stupid by itself.

Zoey waited.

"She does this every night," Xander said. "Not Brazil. Vampires. Demons. Whatever crawls out in Sunnydale. She has Giles and us, but we're mostly carrying pointy sticks and hoping the hospital doesn't ask why somebody has bite marks."

"The White House knows about that now."

"Yeah. That's why it feels worse. You know. Your dad knows. Fitzwallace knows. The mountain knows. Everybody had meetings about what resources I get, what job I should take, which country gets to ask me for things. Buffy has been doing it for years and nobody ever had a meeting about her."

"What does she have? Officially."

"A librarian."

"Giles."

"Yeah. He does everything he can. Research, training, patching people up. But if she gets hurt, he can't take her to some protected hospital and explain Slayer injuries. If she needs backup, it's me and Willow and whoever has a free night. If her jacket gets ripped, she buys another jacket. If a sword breaks, we find money for a sword."

"Does she get paid?"

Xander laughed once. "By who?"

"The Council?"

"They pay Giles. They don't pay Buffy."

"Not even hazard pay?"

"Not even regular pay. She spends all night keeping people from getting eaten, and if she needs money she's supposed to find an after-school job. Somewhere between school and graveyards."

The anger surprised him when it arrived. It was not clean anger aimed only at the Council. It included Snyder, the hospital, every adult who had looked at Buffy's exhaustion and decided it was attitude. It included the White House for learning the truth and still making the first giant machinery about him.

It included his parents. It included every offer that had treated his time as valuable enough to bid on while home remained a place where he had learned to be quiet.

"Sorry," he said. "I didn't call to yell about government policy."

"You aren't yelling."

"I'm angsting at noticeable volume."

"You're allowed."

"Am I?"

"Yes. So am I. We can make it a club."

"Membership requirement is adults making decisions about your life and then acting surprised when you have opinions."

"That's too broad. Half the country qualifies."

"Fine. Also armed escorts or secret satellite equipment."

"Better. Very exclusive. Terrible meetings."

Xander smiled despite himself.

"I'm angry for me too," he said. "That's the part that feels selfish."

"Why?"

"Because Buffy's got the bigger thing. Actual destiny. Chosen girl, one in all the world, nightly death schedule. My stuff happened last week."

"Your life doesn't have to be worse than hers before you're allowed to hate parts of it."

"Tell that to my guilt."

"Your guilt sounds exhausting."

"It contains multitudes."

It’s like, just because you *can* carry something heavy, everyone assumes you’re supposed to," Zoey said. "And once you pick it up, they all walk away because, hey, you’ve got it handled."

Xander stopped picking at his sleeve.

"Yeah."

"Security people say I understand the risks, so I should understand every restriction. Your military people say you can cross continents, so everybody gets to build plans around you. Buffy's Council says she's the Slayer, so apparently that means she doesn't need a salary or a doctor."

"Or a night off."

"Or privacy."

"Or a normal life."

"Like those are rewards you earn after doing the impossible correctly."

The line went quiet between them.

Xander looked through Giles's window at the courtyard below. A neighbor carried groceries from a car. Somebody's television laughed behind a wall. His chemistry book waited on the table, ordinary and unreasonable.

"My dad should hear this," Zoey said.

Xander straightened. "Zoey."

"Not from me. From you and Buffy."

"I was venting."

"You were. You also described a seventeen-year-old doing essential work without medical care, reliable backup, equipment money, or pay. Those can be both things."

"I can't offer Buffy to the government."

"I'm not asking you to. I'm offering to arrange a meeting. You, Buffy, and my father, in person. You make your own case. She makes hers. I get you through the door and then I stop."

"Your dad's the President."

"I had noticed."

"Buffy doesn't love institutions."

"Then she can tell him that."

"She might say no."

"Then the answer is no."

Xander rubbed the back of his neck, the motion automatic. The call had completely changed course. He’d dialed for a friendly ear, for someone who understood the weirdness of shampoo and bodyguards. Instead, Zoey had just casually offered him a door into the Oval Office. A door he hadn't even known existed.

"I have to ask her."

"I know."

"And if she says yes, it's her meeting too. Not me talking about her while she sits there."

"I know."

"And you're not going to tell your dad what we want before we know what we want."

"Xander."

"Right. You know."

"I do."

He could hear the smile in her voice.

"Thanks," he said.

"For what?"

"Listening to the angst."

"It's an exclusive club. We have obligations."

Somebody spoke near Zoey, too muffled for him to make out.

"I have to go," she said. "My detail has located a new hallway to stand in."

"Suspicious-looking hallway?"

"Very. Trench coat and everything."

"Stay on your side of the continent for a while."

"You too."

"I make no promises."

"Apparently neither does Brazil. Call me after you talk to Buffy."

"I will."

The line clicked off.

Xander held the receiver a moment longer before setting it down.

The chemistry book remained open to the same unanswered question.

He closed it.

---

Buffy heard him out without interrupting.

That was worse than if she had interrupted.

They were in the library after school the next day. Giles had locked the doors and put the CLOSED FOR CATALOGING sign in the glass, a phrase that had never once prevented a determined student from trying the handle but gave him grounds to look offended when they did.

Willow had gone to return a computer disk to the lab. Xander waited for her to leave before turning to Buffy. He'd mentioned Zoey's offer to Giles, but this next part had to be hers alone.

"She said she can arrange an in-person meeting," he finished. "You, me, President Bartlet. She gets us through the door. She doesn't make the case for us."

Buffy turned a stake between her fingers. "What case?"

"That's up to us."

"You called the President's daughter to complain about me?"

"I called the President's daughter to complain about me. You came up because my complaints are social and include friends."

Glad to know I made the cut for your social complaints tour.

"I told her the White House knows demons exist, but you still do this with pointy wood and Giles's first-aid box. She said her dad should hear it from us."

"Us."

"If you want. I told her I couldn't say yes for you."

Buffy looked at Giles. "Did you know about this?"

"Not until Xander told me before you arrived."

"And?"

"And I believe being offered access is different from being offered to anyone."

"That's a very Watcher answer."

"It was intended as a Buffy answer."

She put the stake down. "Government help comes with government. They don't send a doctor and forget you exist. They send forms and people who want reports and somebody who decides the forms mean they own the doctor."

"I know," Xander said.

"Do you? You got a watch yesterday. Today Snyder knows you left class, a military duty officer knows where you went, and somebody in Brazil knows how fast you can move a truck. That's help working correctly."

"Also why I asked you instead of telling Zoey yes."

Buffy walked to the cage and back. "What did you ask for?"

"Nothing. I was angsting."

"Professionally?"

"Teenage amateur division."

"And she heard policy."

"She has political ears."

Buffy looked at the table, at the stack of patrol maps, the dented first-aid box, the crossbow Giles had repaired twice because there was no money for another.

"If I go," she said, "I don't become theirs."

"No."

"No government Watcher. No orders about where I patrol."

"No."

"Nobody gets to decide Angel is hostile because vampire goes in the bad column. Nobody takes artifacts because they have a lab. Nobody puts cameras on my house or follows my mother."

"Those sound like things we tell Bartlet."

"And if they say those are the price?"

"We leave."

"You too?"

Xander frowned. "Why wouldn't I?"

"Because you have your own arrangement with them now. Watch, training, rescue calls."

"The arrangement says I decide. It doesn't say I stop being your friend when a meeting gets awkward."

The tension in her shoulders eased, just slightly, as if she'd been braced for a different answer.

Giles removed his glasses. "It may help to define what you would accept, not merely what you would refuse."

"Doctor," Buffy said immediately. "One who doesn't call the police when I show up with a sword wound."

"Transport," Xander added. "So every emergency isn't us borrowing Giles's car."

"Records access," Giles said. "Municipal, historical, police and medical where legally possible. Many threats leave patterns we discover only after the fact."

"And assistance using them," Xander said. "Otherwise we just get buried under better-organized paper. The SGC has an archaeologist on speed-dial."

Giles looked interested despite himself. "An actual archaeologist?"

"Archaeology, ancient languages, comparative mythology, alien pyramids. Daniel contains multitudes."

"Research specialists when the material warrants it," Giles said, adding the point to his notes. "Not merely access."

"Backup," Buffy said. "Requested backup. People who know the difference between helping and charging in."

"Weapons?"

"Equipment," she corrected. "Not just weapons. Clothes. Medical stuff. Replacement stakes, crossbow bolts, whatever gets broken. Cleanup would be nice."

"And pay," Xander said.

Buffy stared at him.

"You work," he said. "Every night. If the government is going to admit the work exists, they can admit eating and clothes cost money."

"I'm not asking the President for an allowance."

"Hazard pay isn't an allowance."

"Since when do you say hazard pay?"

"Since Zoey said it and I realized there was a name for the thing nobody gives you."

Buffy picked up the stake again. The wood pressed grooves into her palm.

"I don't know if I want that."

"Okay."

"I didn't say no."

"Also okay."

She looked at Giles. "What do you think? Really."

"I think the Council has spent centuries treating your calling as an obligation while considering support a matter of discretion. I think any government is capable of reproducing that failure on a much larger budget.
