Some distant part of him knew how this looked. O'Neill would have found the command center, disabled communications, controlled the guards, and chosen an exit which did not require demolishing every expensive object between here and daylight.

Xander was not O'Neill.

He was seventeen. He had spent the day lifting a school off children. The government that asked him to come had waited until his hands were still dirty from its disaster and locked him in a box.

He found the vehicle bay.

Two armored carriers blocked the exterior doors. A web launcher on the first vehicle tracked toward him. The operator was still inside.

Xander crossed the distance, opened the armored roof like a tin lid, and lifted the man out by the back of his body armor. He placed him behind a concrete barrier.

Then he picked up the carrier.

"He thought of the locked door, the ambush. Their bad idea, not his.

He threw it through the unmanned communications array.

The second carrier attempted to reverse. Xander caught its front bumper, lifted until all four wheels left the ground, and placed it on top of the remains of the capture rig. The engine continued running at an offended angle.

The exterior doors began closing additional armored shutters.

Xander went through all of them.

Steel curled outward. Concrete fractured around the frames. One communications mast toppled across an empty parking area. Radar dishes collapsed when their supports tore free from the wall he had just turned into open air and falling debris.

He stopped outside under open sky.

No magic returned him to the room.

No boundary redirected him. No rule cared where the Chinese government wanted him to be. Behind him, the hardened facility had acquired a series of Xander-shaped disagreements extending from the debrief chamber to the mountain air.

Colonel Gao emerged from a separate command entrance with Major Qiao and a dozen soldiers. Qiao looked from the ruined vehicle bay to Xander and went pale.

Gao spoke through him.

"The colonel says this destruction is an attack upon the People's Republic of China."

Xander could hear people moving inside the facility. Frightened, bruised, coughing from dust. Alive. No trapped heartbeats. No serious injuries he could detect.

He looked at Gao.

"I came because your people needed help. I listened to your rescue chief. I saved everyone I could. Then you used that to get me into a room and tried to keep me."

Qiao translated. Gao answered sharply.

"He says examination was necessary for future cooperation."

"Cooperation starts when you ask. It ends when you use people under a collapsed school to arrange an ambush."

"Will you refuse future requests from China?"

Xander looked toward the disaster valley. He remembered the little girl holding his cape and Chief Lin saying you listened.

"No. Innocent people don't become less innocent because their government did this. But I won't assume authorization means trust again."

He lifted from the ground.

Below him, soldiers spread through a facility no longer capable of containing weather.

The pressure from the repeating block still lived in his memory.

That had been terrifying.

This had only made him angry.

* * *

The first secure telephone Xander could reach without landing inside another country that had not invited him was at Kadena Air Base.

The air-traffic controllers had received three different warnings before he arrived, none of which agreed on whether Superman was an allied visitor, a diplomatic emergency, or an unidentified object approaching from the west. They cleared an empty apron and sent a security vehicle to meet him. Xander landed slowly, kept both hands visible, and gave the recognition code Carter had made him memorize.

Five minutes later he stood in a windowless communications room while a captain locked the door from outside.

O'Neill answered the secure line.

"Xander?"

The use of his name nearly undid the last of the anger holding him upright.

"They tried to kidnap me."

O'Neill's voice changed. "Are you hurt?"

"No."

"Are you still in China?"

"Okinawa. Kadena."

"Did anybody come after you?"

"Not that I saw."

"Did you hurt anyone?"

Xander leaned one hand against the desk. The edge creaked. He let go.

"No. I checked. Nobody's dead. Nobody's hurt bad. Some bruises, maybe. Dust. One guy might have a broken wrist, but that happened when he fell getting away from the door. I didn't hit anybody."

"Okay. Stay on the line."

"I wrecked the place."

"We'll get there. Right now, you are safe, you are on a secure American installation, and nobody is dead. Tell me how it started."

Xander began with the school because the school mattered.

He described Lin, the children, the textile workers, and the rescue corridor. He told O'Neill about Major Qiao's maps and the convoy away from the civilians. Then the locked room, gas, restraints, current, and the demand for examination.

"How much gas?" O'Neill asked.

"Enough to fill the room."

"Restraints?"

"Four cables. Net. Floor anchors."

"Weapons?"

"Electrical. Web launcher. Soldiers had rifles, but they didn't fire."

"And you exited."

"Yeah."

"Through the door?"

Xander closed his eyes. "Not the first one."

"How many walls?"

"I'm not sure."

"Estimate."

"Four? Maybe five if the vehicle bay counts."

"Doors?"

"Three."

"Vehicles?"

"Two armored carriers."

O'Neill was quiet.

"One still works," Xander added. "Probably. It's just on top of some other stuff."

"What other stuff?"

"Capture rig. Communications equipment. A radar thing. There were sensors."

Each noun made the event larger. Inside the facility he had seen only obstacles and empty spaces behind them. Spoken aloud, they became rooms full of people, national property, military hardware, and an international incident extending outward from his hands.

"I was angry," he said.

"You had cause."

"That's not the same as it being okay."

"No. It isn't."

Xander opened his eyes.

"What happens now?"

"Now you drink some water, let the base doctor look at you without taking samples, and stay where people on our side can find you. I make calls. You do not make statements, apologize to governments, accept blame, threaten anybody, or fly back across Chinese airspace."

"I wasn't planning to."

"Good. First successful briefing of the evening."

The familiar dryness helped more than reassurance would have.

"Colonel?"

"Still here."

"The block in Sunnydale scared me more."

"We'll talk about that too."

O'Neill kept him on the line until the base physician arrived.

Then Washington began finding out how many problems could fit inside one rescue.

* * *

Leo established four facts before allowing the President to speak to anyone outside the room.

The earthquake was real. The rescue request was legitimate. Civilian witnesses had seen Superman work under Chinese direction and leave only after the rescue chief released him. The containment facility's own surviving systems recorded preparations which began before he entered the country.

"So they can't plausibly call it an unprovoked attack," Leo said.

Elizabeth Weir had been pulled from an Antarctic coordination call and still wore the heavy sweater she used aboard the recovery ship. "Plausibility is not a prerequisite for an official statement."

"Can they sustain one?"

"Not without suppressing their own rescue personnel, explaining a purpose-built containment site, and betting that we won't release evidence. They may accuse him of excessive destruction while avoiding how he entered the facility."

Bartlet paced between his desk and the windows. "He saved thirty-one of their citizens."

"At least," Fitzwallace said.

"Then they drugged him."

"Attempted to."

"They electrocuted him."

"Attempted to."

"I am not finding the distinction soothing, Admiral."

"It was not intended that way, Mr. President."

Ambassador Wen waited in the Roosevelt Room under the supervision of the State Department and two unhappy Secret Service agents. Beijing had provided him one statement denying authorization for any detention and another instructing him not to concede that a detention had occurred. The two messages had arrived nine minutes apart.

In Beijing, the same contradiction was moving upward through ministries which had not known the operation existed.

The regional command described the facility as an emergency-security site and Superman's confinement as a temporary safety measure. Military intelligence described the examination plan as an authorized strategic assessment. The Foreign Ministry asked who had authorized it and received answers which referred to one another in a circle. Nobody wanted responsibility for the decision. Several people wanted credit for the original plan, provided the portion involving its result could be assigned elsewhere.

Colonel Gao's report emphasized the destruction. Major Qiao's first statement recorded that Superman had complied with every rescue instruction, entered the convoy voluntarily, and refused examination before using force. Chief Engineer Lin submitted the names of thirty-one people recovered alive and threatened to speak directly to provincial officials if his rescue crews were accused of participating in an ambush they had known nothing about.

That testimony was already a political fact. The people of two damaged villages knew who had lifted the school. Relief workers had watched the military convoy take him away. Any public charge against Superman risked producing public questions about why a successful rescue ended inside a hardened military installation.

The failed unit had exposed more than intent. Its surviving records listed every assumption Chinese analysts had made about Superman's breathing, nervous system, conductivity, leverage, restraint tolerances, and willingness to use force around human beings. The operation had disproved most of the physical assumptions and confirmed the last one. He had destroyed the machinery while protecting the people operating it.

None of those conclusions made the meeting waiting for Ambassador Wen easier.

Leo said, "Wen may not know who approved it. This could be central leadership, regional command, intelligence, or a faction that thought success would create permission retroactively."

"It was a Chinese military facility," Bartlet said.

"Which makes China responsible. It doesn't make the ambassador omniscient."

"Fine. We will be furious with institutional precision."

Fitzwallace placed the rescue protocol on the desk. "We share responsibility for one part."

Bartlet stopped pacing.

"Choose the next words carefully."

We confirmed the disaster and got authorization. Then we sent a seventeen-year-old volunteer in with nothing but a phone summary and our trust in the host government. We gave him no comms, no observation, no extraction plan. There was nobody on our side empowered to even know he'd been captured until he tore himself out."

The President's anger widened without diminishing.

"Fix it."

"Future international requests require verified scope, an independent communications link, continuous observation where politically possible, and an extraction plan which does not rely on the government making the request. If terms change, someone here has authority to withdraw him immediately."

"He can fly."

A way home isn't just about flight. It's about knowing the route is clear, having a friendly base to land on, and a voice on the line who knows you're overdue. He had none of that."

Weir nodded. "We also need a rule for humanitarian requests from governments we have reason to distrust. Refusing every request punishes civilians for official conduct. Accepting unchanged invites repetition."

"He already told them he'd still come," Leo said.

Bartlet looked at him. "Of course he did."

The sadness in those four words lasted only a moment.

"Bring Wen in."

The ambassador entered carrying a folder he did not open. He looked more tired than he had during the Enterprise negotiations and less certain of the ground beneath him.

"Mr. President."

"Mr. Ambassador, Superman entered your country today at your government's request. He recovered survivors from a collapsed school. Afterward, officers of your armed forces confined him, assaulted him, and attempted to compel examination."

Wen's face remained composed. "My government has not confirmed this characterization."

"Your government built a very expensive room for an event it has not confirmed. The room is less expensive now."

"There has been extensive damage to a military emergency facility."

"There has. We have a seventeen-year-old in Okinawa trying to remember how many walls he broke after your officers locked him inside them. You and I are going to discuss both facts, and neither will be used to erase the other."

Wen glanced toward Weir. She offered him no rescue.

"I have requested full instructions from Beijing," he said.

"Request faster."

The meeting lasted forty-three minutes. It produced no apology, no public statement, and no agreement about the word detention. It did produce a promise to preserve the facility records, access to the civilian rescue testimony, and a private understanding that neither government would release images before Beijing determined whose career had just become available for sacrifice.

Containment of the incident was not silence. It was active work performed across twelve time zones by people who understood that one leaked photograph could turn a failed abduction into either an arms race or a war.

Other governments learned anyway.

Some learned from satellite images of a new opening in a hardened Chinese facility. Some learned because China abruptly asked technical questions about high-tension materials. Some learned from people at the rescue site who had seen Superman arrive and disappear into a military convoy.

Files opened in capitals around the world.

Several changed their headings from recruitment to cooperation.

Not all of them.

* * *

Chegwidden received the preliminary incident report twenty minutes after agreeing to take the Sunnydale mission.

Fitzwallace remained in his office, using the secure telephone while Chegwidden read.

"No," Chegwidden said.

"I haven't asked anything."

"You're going to ask whether my new command can cover him too. The answer is no."

"The President may want coordination."

"Coordination can be offered through the White House and SGC. Summers's support command does not become a global Superman office because two teenagers suffer from related adult failures. Scope expands explicitly or it doesn't expand."

Fitzwallace nodded. "Good answer."

"You knew it was the answer."

"I wanted to hear you say it."

Chegwidden turned to the final page. "The principle applies. Voluntary extraordinary responders require somebody watching the larger field. He could escape under his own power. He couldn't know the government had changed the terms until the door closed."

"We'll build the new rescue protocol."

"Send me a copy. My watch officers may be able to offer analysis when Sunnydale and Harris overlap, but no standing tasking."

"Agreed."

Chegwidden tapped the casualty line. Zero fatalities. Zero serious injuries. Extensive infrastructure loss.

"He kept track of the people."

"Yes."

"At seventeen, furious, under attack. That's not nothing."

"It also can't be the entire control system."

"No."

Chegwidden looked at the two personnel files again.

"Get O'Neil and Urgayle here. I want to know how they think before I give either of them people."

* * *

Xander returned to Sunnydale after midnight.

Buffy was on the library steps before he landed. Giles, Willow, and Ethan waited inside. Nobody asked whether he was injured. They had heard enough through O'Neill to know the answer.

Buffy looked at his torn, smoke-stained uniform.

"How many walls?"

"O'Neill asked that."

"Did you answer him?"

"I estimated."

"How many?"

"Four or five."

"That's a range, not an estimate."

"There was a big vehicle door. Architecturally, I don't know where we stand."

She opened the library door for him. "Come on."

The iron survey plate lay inside a chalk boundary on the main table. Ethan had surrounded it with scraps of paper, bowls of salt, and three objects Xander did not recognize. Giles had the municipal maps open again. Willow held a mug in both hands and looked awake through an act of personal hostility toward sleep.

Xander changed in the office and returned wearing ordinary clothes.

Buffy gave him one of Mrs. Landingham's reserve cookies. It had survived two flights, a full school day, and several hours of patrol in her bag. The edge was broken, a small casualty of a very long day.

He ate it anyway.

"I'm not going to tell you they didn't deserve to have you break out," she said.

"But."

But I'm also not going to throw you a party for remodeling their base with your fists.

"Facility."

"That correction is not helping."

Xander leaned against the circulation desk. "They put me in a room. Gas, cables, nets. And after the block, it was easy. I knew exactly what all of it was. I knew I could leave whenever I wanted."

"So you left through five walls."

"Four or five."

"Still not helping."

He looked at the wrapped survey plate. "That street... that scared me." He finally met their eyes. "In China, I knew I could leave. Here, I could have gotten angrier forever and it wouldn't have mattered. I could see where I wanted to go, hear the people in the car, and nothing I did changed where the spell said I had to be."

Giles removed his glasses. "Your restraint in China matters. You identified every person near the route you took and chose not to strike them."

"Nobody got hurt."

"Not seriously. That is worth recognizing. It cannot be the whole of control."

Xander went still. "What does that mean?

"It means your senses are extraordinary but your judgment remains human. You cannot build safety around the belief that while enraged you will always see every person, understand every secondary consequence, and choose correctly before acting."

"Then what do I build it around?"

"Procedure before anger," Giles said. "A contact who knows where you are. A reliable extraction route even if you believe you won't require one. Rules decided while calm about what you will and won't destroy. People authorized to speak to you when your own judgment narrows."

"A team," Willow said.

"Yes. Not because a team can physically restrain him. Because restraint is more than a contest of strength."

"So I was supposed to stay in the box?"

"No," Buffy said.

The word cut cleanly through the room.

"You were supposed to get out. They betrayed you. They used hurt people to get close enough to try taking you apart. You get to be angry. And now when you're angry, buildings can lose. Both are true."

Xander stared down at his hands. "I didn't even have to try."

She pulled out the chair across from him and turned it around before lowering herself onto it, arms folded over the back.

"I burned down a school gym," she said.

Xander looked up. "There were vampires."

"There were. It was also a school gym. People who weren't there had to deal with what I did. Fire department, teachers, my parents. I still think setting the fire was the right choice. I don't think that means every part after it stopped mattering."

"You got expelled."

"Because nobody knew what really happened. Which was unfair. Also, building still burned down."

"Are you comparing international kidnapping to Hemery?"

I know what it's like. You make a choice that saves your life, and everyone else uses it to prove you're the monster. I'm not gonna help them pin that on you. But I'm not gonna let you pretend you didn't leave a hole in the wall, either. They started it, but you finished it."

Xander breathed out slowly. "That's annoyingly balanced."

"I contain multitudes."

"Now you're just stealing my material."

"Apparently it's everybody's material."

Willow lowered her mug. "Would you go back? If they called again, I mean."

Xander did not need to ask which they she meant.

"If people were trapped?"

"Yes."

"I told Gao I would."

Buffy studied him. "Even after this."

"Chief Lin didn't put me in that room. The kids didn't. If I say governments only get their people rescued when the government behaves, I'm making the same choice Gao did. Using civilians to get what I want from somebody in charge."

"That's a very mature answer," Giles said.

"I hate it."

"Most mature answers have that defect."

"Next time I want somebody listening," Xander said. "The whole time. And if the safe exit changes, I want to know before I walk into the mountain they built around the trap."

"That's not cowardice," Buffy said.

"Didn't say it was."

"Your face did."

He rubbed both hands over it. "My face is also seventeen. It contains multitudes."

"That's why we're having the conversation," she said.

Ethan unwrapped the iron marker.

"If the mutual improvement society has concluded its opening remarks, this deserves attention."

"You found something?" Willow asked.

"I found absence. No binding remains on the plate. The working released it cleanly once municipal possession ended. No backlash, no attempt to preserve itself, no secondary injury."

Giles understood first. "It was designed to be solved."

"Or at least designed to permit a solution. There is a difference, but not one likely to comfort us."

Ethan traced a finger above the old seal without touching it.

"This is professional work. Juridical construction, civic authority, layered carriers, explicit transfer conditions. Wolfram & Hart grade."

"You know it was them?" Buffy asked.

"I know the school. Los Angeles has several practitioners who confuse contract law with a moral philosophy. The residue is consistent. It is not proof."

Giles placed the 1899 map beside the older survey description. "The caster had access to a boundary which predates most surviving town records."

"And knew exactly where we'd look," Willow said. "The mailbox and storm drain were clues."

"They were instruments," Ethan corrected. "We were the subject."

He looked around the table.

"Think about what we just did. We were tested, and we showed them our playbook. They know you can sense their work now, Xander. They know I'm training you." He looked at Willow. "They know you can read a spell's architecture." His gaze shifted to Giles. "And that you'll go straight to the historical record. They saw Buffy take command, and they saw what it takes to make you hesitate.

"We beat it," Xander said.

"Yes. It expected us to."

The room became colder without any change in temperature.

Willow asked, "Then what happens next?"

"Something less considerate than a visible survey marker."

Xander looked at the plate. "Magic can stop me without hurting me."

"Magic can impose a condition your skin was never asked to resist," Giles said. "Invulnerability is irrelevant if nothing attempts to penetrate it."

"Great. Superman, defeated by zoning law."

"Not defeated," Buffy said.

"I ran around one block until you fixed it."

"We fixed it. You were the only person who could reach the marker without becoming extra-crispy. Willow found the rule. Giles found the right anchor. Ethan stopped you from making it worse. Angel and I got the family out."

"That's a lot of people required for me to cross a street."

"That's a team."

He looked at her.

Buffy glanced toward the uniform hidden in Giles's office. "China proves you need people around you when you can break everything. Tonight proves you need people around you when you can't. Neither one makes you useless."

Xander let that settle.

"Do teams get more cookies?"

Buffy checked the waxed packet. "Team consumed the final cookie."

"Mrs. Landingham would want us to requisition more."

"Mrs. Landingham has jurisdiction," Willow said.

Giles put his glasses back on. "Can we return to the magical assault on the town?"

"I contain multitudes," Buffy said.

Ethan almost smiled, then looked again at the dead survey plate.

"Enjoy the victory," he told them. "You earned it. But understand what it purchased."

"What?" Xander asked.

"The next working will be built for the people you proved yourselves to be tonight."



Chapter 22: Chapter 22



Xander's knuckles did not look like they had gone through five reinforced walls.

Buffy checked them anyway.

They stood in Giles's office before first period while the secure line connected to Colorado. Xander held the receiver between his shoulder and ear. Buffy turned his right hand over, inspected the unmarked palm, then repeated the process with his left.

"I told you I'm fine."

"You also told us four or five walls."

"Architecture is subjective."

"Skin isn't."

The Chinese cables had scorched his uniform and the electrical traps had blackened the concrete around his feet, but his body kept no mark of it—no cuts, bruises, or burns. His mind, however, kept everything. He'd slept for forty-three minutes, and each time he closed his eyes, the debriefing-room door locked again, the sound echoing until it became the repeating block, trapping him beside the same mailbox forever while children screamed somewhere he couldn't reach.

The school bell would ring in seven minutes.

He had a history quiz in second period.

The telephone clicked. "O'Neil."

"I'm here. Buffy too."

"Good. First, you're not being asked to apologize for leaving the facility."

Xander had prepared for anger. He had prepared for someone to tell him China deserved it. O'Neil offered neither, and that left him without the shield for either conversation.

"Okay."

"Second, wrecking the facility demonstrated why we need to work on what happens after somebody earns your anger. That's not punishment and it's not praise."

"Giles said procedure before anger."

"Giles has a point, even if he says it from behind a wall of tweed."

"He's right here."

Giles looked up from the doorway. "Good morning, Colonel."

"Morning, Giles. The United States owns its part. We handed Xander a location and proof of diplomatic authorization. We did not give him continuous overwatch, independent communications, a safe extraction option, or a person empowered to notice the host government had changed the mission."

"You couldn't have pulled me out," Xander said.

"Maybe not physically. We could have warned you that the departure corridor hadn't changed. We could have told you the convoy was taking you off the agreed route. We could have had aircraft, diplomatic contacts, and a secure landing site ready before you needed them. A guy who can fly still needs someone to notice he's missing."

Buffy released Xander's hands. "And next time somebody knows what happens if the rescue turns into a trap."

"There will be a next time," O'Neil said. "Not necessarily China. Somebody will try again."

Xander looked through the office window at students moving toward class. "So the lesson is never get angry."

"No," Buffy said.

O'Neil said it at almost the same time.

Buffy leaned against Giles's desk. "The anger was earned. That's what makes it useful to anybody trying to control you. They don't have to fake it. They just arrange for you to be completely justified while cameras are running and governments are watching."

"That is disturbingly close to the briefing I have in front of me," O'Neil said.

"I contain multitudes."

"Apparently."

Xander rubbed his eyes. "I don't want Washington deciding if I get to answer a call."

"Not on the table," O'Neil said. "You decide. The review is about what we owe you if you say yes, what you need to know before arrival, and what everyone does if the terms change."

"And if I ignore the plan?"

"Then we adapt. Voluntary doesn't mean unaccountable, but accountable doesn't mean owned. We will probably spend several thousand pages discovering the space between those words."

"Lots and lots of very official paperwork?"

"I knew you'd understand."

The warning bell rang.

Giles said, "Xander still requires sleep."

"He does," O'Neil agreed. "He also requires a review at sixteen hundred local. Secure line. Carter will have the receiver logs."

"I'll be here."

"And Xander?"

"Yeah?"

"Thirty-one people walked out because of you. That happened before any of this, and it still happened after. Don't let them rewrite it."

The line disconnected.

Xander remained still for a moment.

Buffy opened the office door. "Come on. You can have an international crisis after history."

"I didn't study."

"Excellent. A normal crisis."

They made it to class before Snyder found a reason to stop them.

Xander took the quiz with hands that looked completely untouched.

He knew the material. Giles had made him review it on the flight to Washington, and Willow had tested him again before the wildfire call. The first question asked for three causes of the French Revolution.

Xander read it six times.

Each time his mind supplied the sound of locking bars.

He wrote taxation, food scarcity, and inequality. Someone dropped a metal ruler behind him. The sharp crack on the tile was too close to an anchor firing into concrete. Xander flinched, losing four minutes as he stared at the page, his pen frozen in his hand.

Willow glanced over. Xander shook his head before she could ask, finished the remaining answers, and turned in the quiz at the bell.

Snyder waited outside the classroom.

"Mr. Harris."

"Principal Snyder. Always a comfort when authority lurks near an educational exit."

"You left school grounds without authorization during third period on Friday."

Brazil felt as distant as China and equally impossible to explain.

"Family emergency."

"Your family seems to treat international incidents as a hobby. Detention. Today."

"I have an appointment after school."

"Then tomorrow."

Xander accepted the slip. He could demolish a hardened military facility. He could not, however, make detention disappear without explaining to the world why a Sunnydale High student needed to.

Buffy caught up near the stairs and read the yellow paper.

"How's normal crisis?"

"Rescheduled for tomorrow. Very accommodating."

"You okay?"

He looked back at the classroom door. "No metal rulers in the rescue protocol."

She understood anyway. "Add them under lessons learned."

They went to chemistry.

* * *

Ambassador Wen returned to the White House carrying more information and less room to use it.

Leo began the meeting by reading the verified sequence into the record.

"At zero nine fourteen local time, the People's Republic requested humanitarian assistance. At ten oh two, Superman entered the authorized corridor. From ten nineteen until fourteen forty-seven, he operated under Chief Engineer Lin Wei's direction. Thirty-one living civilians were recovered. At fifteen twelve, Major Qiao directed him into a military convoy on the false representation that his departure corridor had changed. At fifteen twenty-eight, he entered Facility Seventeen. The capture systems were prepared before his arrival."

Wen did not interrupt.

"At fifteen thirty-one, Colonel Gao ordered him to submit to examination. Sedative gas, electrical restraint cables, mechanical anchors, and layered barriers had already been deployed. At fifteen thirty-four, Superman exited through the western side of the facility. No person was killed. Three suffered injuries requiring treatment. None were struck by him."

Leo closed the report.

"Does your government dispute any part of that sequence?"

"My government disputes the characterization of the facility's purpose."

Elizabeth Weir said, "Then it can provide the construction orders."

Wen looked at her. "Some records concern national security."

"You used humanitarian access to attempt custody of a foreign minor. Invoking national security doesn't change the fact that you tried to abduct him."

"Superman entered as an independent actor of uncertain legal status."

"Xander Harris entered as a sixteen-year-old American citizen invited for a rescue," Bartlet said. "You don't get to change which identity counts after the door locks."

Wen's composure thinned. "A Chinese military installation has been destroyed."

Fitzwallace folded his hands on the table. "If Superman had killed or seriously injured your personnel, this would be a different conversation. The fact that he did not matters. The destroyed gas delivery system, capture rig, restraints, armored vehicles, and blast doors do not make your planned detention disappear. They document it."

"You are asking China to accept destruction of sovereign property without consequence."

"No," Bartlet said. "We're refusing to discuss the broken lock as if nobody built the cage."

Wen opened his folder. Beijing had identified Facility Seventeen as a regional military-intelligence project operating under authority which remained under internal review. Colonel Gao had been relieved pending investigation. Major Qiao and Chief Lin were available for controlled interviews. Raw recordings would be preserved, though access remained disputed.

It was not an admission. It was no longer a denial.

Bartlet made no threat. He asked for the names of every official who approved the containment plan, construction, and use of humanitarian authorization. He required preservation of all records, access to rescue witnesses without military coaching, and written guarantees governing any future request.

"Superman's position should also be clear," Weir said. "He will not refuse future humanitarian assistance merely because the civilians are Chinese. He will not again rely on Chinese authorization as his only protection entering or leaving the country."

Wen read the sentence beneath the sentence. China had failed to frighten him away from its people. It had succeeded in making every future rescue an internationally supervised judgment on Chinese trust.

"Public release of allegations would complicate cooperation," he said.

"Public release of the rescue footage would complicate your alternative," Leo replied.

The footage already existed in too many hands. Children had emerged from the school beneath a red cape. Villagers knew which direction the convoy had taken afterward. An official story that began with attack would have to erase the rescue first.

China needed the scandal contained. So did Washington. Neither need made the event smaller.

Wen closed his folder. "I will convey your requirements."

"Convey the deadline too," Bartlet said.

"Forty-eight hours may not be sufficient."

"It was enough time to build a trap."

The meeting moved behind closed doors and stayed there.

* * *

Chegwidden gave Lieutenant Jordan O'Neil and Command Master Chief John James Urgayle the unclassified version first.

It lasted thirty seconds.

"A civilian support mission in Southern California," he said. "Small forward element, full reach-back from Coronado, unusual threat environment, strict limits on independent action."

Urgayle looked at the red-bordered folders on the table. "And the classified version?"

"Vampires are real. So are demons. A sixteen-year-old girl has spent two years providing the primary armed response to both. The President wants her supported, not recruited."

O'Neil held Chegwidden's gaze for a long beat, searching for the punchline. Finding none, she opened the folder. Neither she nor Urgayle wasted time on disbelief; they processed the impossible by turning it into procedure, reading the contact summaries and casualty estimates.

"Who wrote this consent language?" she asked.

"White House counsel."

"It says participation is voluntary. It doesn't say refusal cannot affect protection, medical access, or compensation."

"You're here because you noticed."

O'Neil looked up. "You label a young woman uniquely capable, drop her into a situation no one else can survive, and then tell her every choice is voluntary after you've removed all her other options. That's not a choice. It's a cage."

Chegwidden knew she had. Her own file contained a Senate program, a manufactured test case, and a career nearly reduced to proof that a political experiment had been attempted. It also contained the names of people who learned too late that Jordan O'Neil treated consent as an operational fact rather than a ceremonial signature.

"Fix the language," he said.

Urgayle tapped the field-authority paragraph. "Contact authority?"

"Summers determines Slayer priorities and supernatural field judgment. O'Neil commands Navy personnel."

"If our intelligence finds a threat before she does?"

"Develop the assessment. Offer the intelligence, personnel, equipment, transport, or proposed operation. State risks and basis. She decides."

"If she refuses?"

"We document, reassess, and remain available."

"If she's wrong?"

"Same answer."

"Mass casualties developing?"

"Clear, immediate, and independently reviewable. You may act to prevent them. That exception is not permission to rename uncertainty."

Urgayle nodded once. "Who reviews?"

"Me. Every use."

"Acceptable."

O'Neil turned another page. "No DRI personnel, facilities, records, or tasking."

"Correct."

"Does DRI know?"

"Only that it doesn't own the mission."

"That won't make them less interested."

"No. It makes our recordkeeping more important."

Chegwidden outlined the core of the mission. They would provide medical care that couldn't be used as leverage and secure transport she could trust. They'd be her logistics: supplies, replacement clothing, compensation. They'd handle intelligence, research, and cleanup. Most importantly, they'd provide personnel who understood that a horned civilian wasn't automatically a target.

"Not a SEAL team," O'Neil said.

"A SEAL team may be one tool. The mission is the infrastructure that knows when not to use it."

Urgayle looked at the personnel appendix. "Permission to cut this roster?"

"I expected you to."

"Half these people volunteered because they heard special operations and classified."

"Remove anyone who's here for the prestige. We need people who will do the work, not chase a commendation."

"That'll be more than half."

"Recruit better."

O'Neil closed her folder. "I want her refusal protected in every annex, including finance. No pay per patrol, no bonus per kill, no condition that she accept our equipment or personnel. Medical care cannot require samples unrelated to treatment. Intelligence cannot use threat collection as cover to surveil her friends."

"Put it in writing."

"And if an agency objects?"

"Bring it to me."

"If the White House objects?"

"Bring it to me louder."

Urgayle asked, "Does Summers know our names?"

"Not yet. No one deploys before she is briefed."

"Good."

Chegwidden regarded both officers. "I did not summon you because this is easy to explain. I summoned you because the government has already spent years accepting the work of a child while avoiding the duties which would make the arrangement visible. This command either corrects that failure or becomes its next layer of cover."

O'Neil said, "When do we start?"

Urgayle was already crossing names off the roster.

* * *

By fourteen hundred, a secure planning room at Coronado contained representatives from seven disciplines and no agreement about what to call themselves.

Coronado was not a symbolic choice. It put the command close enough to reach Sunnydale by road when aircraft would draw attention, close enough to move a trauma patient quickly, and inside a naval complex capable of supplying operators, communications, medicine, transport, and logistics without inventing each function from nothing. It also kept the flag headquarters outside a town whose police, mayor, and institutions could not yet be trusted.

The forward element would be small because Buffy had asked for support, not occupation. The command behind it would be large enough that small did not mean alone.

Chegwidden solved the problem by removing the proposed unit name from the board.

"You can have an identity when you have a functioning watch floor. Until then, you're a room full of section leads."

The operations commander looked wounded. The finance officer looked relieved.

Chegwidden drew three boxes.

BUFFY SUMMERS: SLAYER PRIORITIES.

O'NEIL: DEPLOYED NAVY PERSONNEL.

CHEGWIDDEN: RESOURCES, CONFLICT, MANDATE.

"These authorities overlap," he said. "They do not consume one another. Summers decides what Slayer work requires. Lieutenant O'Neil commands every person we send. I control the resources and settle disputes. Nobody converts logistical dependence into tactical authority over Summers."

The intelligence lead asked, "Are we permitted to collect inside Sunnydale?"

"On threats and patterns relevant to protection. Not on Summers or her associates as targets."

"Her associates include a vampire."

"Then your analysts will learn to write more precise collection requirements."

O'Neil placed a four-part offer form on the table. "Every proactive offer states the evidence, proposed support, known risks, and her right to decline. If she says no, the file does not quietly become an operation under a different authority."

"Unless mass casualties are imminent," the operations commander said.

"Clear and immediate," Chegwidden replied. "Every use reviewed by me. If you find yourself explaining afterward that casualties might eventually have become possible, you did not meet the exception."

The medical planner had three folders open. "We can establish treatment through a Navy physician under a protected patient number. No ordinary billing, no diagnosis in a shared insurance database, and no reporting outside the compartment except where immediate safety law requires it."

"Parental consent?" O'Neil asked.

"Emergency treatment is manageable. Continuing treatment is not. Her mother currently knows none of this. We can protect confidentiality, but we can't falsify the patient into a legal nonperson every time she needs care."

"What happens if a vampire follows her home?"

The planner paused. "That's operations."

"It's household medicine if her mother becomes the casualty. Does Joyce Summers know not to invite strangers inside? Does she know who to call if her daughter arrives unconscious? Can she consent to evacuation? Can she recognize coercion or a threat using Buffy as leverage?"

The intelligence lead said, "We can establish covert protection."

"Outside the house," O'Neil replied. "You cannot make an uninformed parent follow a plan she has never heard."

Chegwidden looked around the table. "Put the question to Summers. Do not answer it around her. State what we can protect temporarily and what we cannot."

The finance officer cleared his throat. "Hazard pay presents classification and employment problems."

"Solve them."

"We can establish a protected compensation trust under classified civil-defense authority. Initial payment can recognize prior service. Continuing disbursements would be periodic, not task-based. No enlistment, no federal employment classification, no payment per contact or operation."

"Usable when?"

"If counsel approves the authority, forty-eight hours."

"Twenty-four," Chegwidden said.

"Admiral, the account alone requires identity verification and a trustee arrangement for a minor."

"Then identify a lawful trustee who cannot redirect the funds or condition access. Bring Summers options. Do not spend three weeks perfecting a promise while she spends her own money replacing a coat torn on patrol."

Logistics began a list: ordinary clothing in Buffy's sizes, boots, stakes, crossbow bolts, first-aid material, evidence containers, secure lockers, portable lighting, radios, and a petty-cash mechanism which did not require an after-action report for every ruined shirt.

Communications proposed a twenty-four-hour duty number and a small encrypted handset. Transport identified one helicopter package, ground vehicles, and access to fixed-wing medical evacuation. Research requested municipal data feeds, archival support, and a path to Dr. Jackson for ancient-language and archaeological questions.

The Chinese report changed every movement plan.

No responder entered a host-controlled environment without continuous contact. No transport plan depended on the authority requesting assistance. Someone outside the operating area tracked location, expected movement, and changes to the agreed terms. Every extraction package named a person empowered to initiate it.

"Does that include Superman?" communications asked.

"Not under this command," Chegwidden said. "We coordinate if White House or SGC channels request it. We do not annex a global mission because the lesson is useful. Scope changes come through written authority."

He made each section state what it could not yet provide.

Medical had no cleared surgeon in Southern California. Intelligence lacked reliable classification for nonhuman contacts. Logistics could supply conventional equipment but had no validated source for magical weapons. Communications could not guarantee satellite coverage inside known distortions. Transport had no safe landing survey for Sunnydale.

The admissions covered half the board.

"Good," Chegwidden said.

The operations commander looked at him. "Good?"

"Honest gaps can be closed. Ceremonial readiness kills people."

At sixteen ten, O'Neil took the medical question to Sunnydale.

* * *

Buffy's coat had gained a six-inch tear under the left arm.

Her shoulder had gained a matching bruise, deep purple around a scrape she had cleaned in the school bathroom. She had completed a geometry worksheet during lunch, forgotten an English response paper in her locker, and slept through enough chemistry to make the teacher ask whether she was ill.

None of it qualified as an emergency.

That was how it kept happening.

Giles examined the shoulder in his office while Willow attempted to repair the coat with a sewing kit which had once belonged to the library's home-economics shelf.

"You should rest it," Giles said.

"I will. While patrolling. Very restful walking."

"The joint may be strained."

"Can still move it."

She proved this and winced only at the top of the motion.

She winced at the top of the motion. Xander looked up from the history book he hadn't absorbed. "That wince says doctor.

"That wince says ice pack. A doctor means forms, questions from Mom, and a cover story for getting hit by a tire iron in a cemetery."

Willow pulled the thread too hard. The coat seam puckered.

"Sorry."

"It's okay. Mom already thinks I ruin clothes recreationally."

Giles's secure telephone rang.

O'Neil introduced herself and asked to speak directly to Buffy. Giles put the call on the small conference speaker Carter had sent.

"Admiral Chegwidden has directed me to brief you before any personnel enter Sunnydale," O'Neil said. "The command is still forming. We have a medical question which affects immediate planning."

"Will it involve needles?"

"Not this one. Who protects your mother if a threat follows you home?"

Buffy stopped moving her shoulder.

O'Neil continued in the same level tone. "Who informs or evacuates her if you're incapacitated? Does she know not to invite an unknown person across the threshold? Can she identify a supernatural threat using you as leverage? If you require emergency treatment, who explains where you are and obtains consent?"

"We keep patrol away from my house."

"Can you guarantee an enemy does?"

Darla had crossed the Summers threshold because Joyce had invited her. Buffy remembered arriving home to find a vampire drinking coffee with her mother, safety breached by ordinary politeness and a rule Joyce had never been allowed to know.

She had always filed that memory under danger to herself.

Now she saw Joyce opening the door after Buffy failed to come home. Joyce answering a telephone call from something which knew Buffy's name. Joyce refusing an evacuation because armed strangers could not explain why. Joyce making medical decisions from a story built to hide the injury instead of treat it.

Her secrecy had not kept Joyce outside the danger.

It had left her blind inside it.

"We can provide discreet observation outside the home for a limited period," O'Neil said. "We can flag immediate threats and protect records. We cannot create an informed safety plan inside a house when the owner doesn't know the danger exists. I'm not telling you what decision to make. I'm telling you the gap."

Buffy looked at the ripped coat in Willow's hands.

"Thank you," she said. "Anything else?"

O'Neil described the command authorities, her right to decline, and the initial communication plan. Buffy asked three questions about surveillance and one about Angel. O'Neil answered each without evasion.

When the call ended, Buffy pulled her coat back on.

"Buffy," Giles began.

"You're coming with me."

"To patrol?"

"Home. I'm telling Mom. You explain vampires, Slayers, and the Council. All of it. You were the adult who knew. So you get to be the adult who explains it."

Giles went pale but reached for his coat.

Willow stood. "Do you want me?"

"Not tonight. She needs to hear me before this becomes a group intervention."

Buffy moved toward the doors.

Xander saw the next problem before Giles did.

He could see it already: Joyce, trying to process vampires and magic. Buffy could punch a wall and Giles could quote his books, but how would they prove this wasn't all just some story? A fantasy cooked up by adults to explain away a troubled girl they couldn't handle?

"She'll need somebody outside the Council," he said.

Buffy stopped.

"Not to tell her for you. Just to confirm it. Somebody she can't write off as part of Giles's secret club."

Giles did not object.

Buffy's face tightened as if the whole decision might collapse if anyone asked her to plan it.

Xander did not ask.

"I know where to get that," he said. "Go."

He was already moving toward Giles's office.

Buffy looked back once. "Xander."

"You do your part. I'll get backup for the impossible part."

She nodded.

Buffy and Giles left through the library doors. Xander changed identities and went through the roof.

* * *

Superman arrived over the White House lawn slowly, visibly, and exactly inside the approach lane the Secret Service had given him after his first visit.

That did not make the detail happy.

It made them organized.

Agents moved below him. A radio call reached the roof post. Xander hovered where instructed until an agent opened the cleared landing area, then descended with both hands visible and waited for identity confirmation.

"Unscheduled visit," the lead agent said.

"I know. I need to speak to the President. Personal and urgent, but nobody is under attack."

The distinction caused three additional radio calls. Xander surrendered the active costume receiver for a scan but not removal, repeated his recognition code, and followed the escort inside.

Mrs. Landingham met him before anyone decided which waiting room could safely contain an unexpected Superman.

She looked at his face once.

"You look like you're frightened for someone."

"Buffy. And her mom. Not attack-frightened. Conversation-frightened."

"Does the President know you're coming?"

"No."

"Of course not."

She turned to the agent. "The President has seven minutes before the economic briefing."

"Mrs. Landingham, we need to clear the contact."

"Then clear it while walking."

She did not ask Xander to explain Buffy's life in the corridor. She put him in the small room adjoining the Oval, gave him water, and went through the next door.

Bartlet appeared less than a minute later.

"Is Buffy in immediate danger?"

"No, sir. Neither is Joyce. Not a new danger."

"Then what happened?"

Xander told him about O'Neil's question. How did a support command protect a household when the parent did not know what could follow her daughter home? Buffy had understood the answer before anyone finished asking. She had ended the call, taken Giles, and gone straight home.

"She asked you to come here?" Bartlet asked.

"No."

"Does she know you're here?"

"She knows I said I'd get backup for the impossible part. She didn't stop to plan what that meant."

Bartlet's expression became careful. "Are you helping Buffy, or deciding what her family needs without her?"

The question hit exactly where Xander had known it might.

"I'm not asking you to reveal anything. Buffy's doing the telling. I'm not asking you to tell Joyce how to react, or when to forgive her, or whether Buffy should have told her sooner. I don't get to decide any of that."

"What are you asking?"

"Don't make Buffy prove herself too."

Bartlet waited.

"Joyce has to believe vampires are real. Then that Buffy has powers. Then that Slayer isn't a story Giles put in her head because she's scared or acting out or got in trouble at Hemery. Buffy tried to tell her parents before. They had her committed. If Joyce thinks this is Giles manipulating her, Giles can't prove Giles isn't manipulating her. He's part of the Council."

"And the Council is responsible for the system which left her unsupported."

"Yeah. He knows the facts and he should be there, but Joyce deserves somebody who doesn't need her to trust the Council first."

Bartlet moved toward his desk. The economic briefing had ceased to exist.

"What can we independently confirm?"

"That vampires and demons exist. That Buffy has strength and reflexes normal people don't. That she's fought them, saved people, and stopped things the government now knows were real. That you're building support because what she does is real. That nobody's recruiting her or taking her away."

"We cannot certify the theology."

"No. We can't prove every Council story about one girl in every generation, destiny, sacred calling, any of that. And we can't answer why Buffy lied. That's between them. But we can stop Joyce from having to choose between believing her daughter and believing the entire world she thought she knew."

Bartlet looked at him for a long moment.

"After four days as Superman," Xander said, "I got military doctors, satellites, and a panic button on my wrist. Buffy's been doing this for years, explaining away injuries and buying her own replacement clothes. The government finally admits she's not crazy, and now she has to prove it to her mom all by herself?

"No," Bartlet said.

"I thought maybe a secure call. After Buffy tells her. You confirm the facts, give Joyce a cleared number for questions, maybe send something official she can verify."

"What will she ask first?"

Xander thought of Joyce Summers at the kitchen counter, trying to build order around a daughter whose life kept refusing it.

"She'll want to know if Buffy's telling the truth. If the danger's real. If 'Slayer' is a real thing or just some story Giles made up. She'll ask why no adults helped, and if you're taking over now. And... she's going to ask how a mother doesn't know this about her own kid. You can't answer that one."

"No."

"Neither can Buffy. Not tonight."

Bartlet looked toward the photographs on the credenza. His daughters smiled out from several ages. Xander watched something in the set of Bartlet's jaw go quiet, though his shoulders never dropped.

"A telephone call can authenticate a fact," Bartlet said. "A document can establish authority. Neither can sit across from a mother and tell her the adults in charge knew her child was drowning and cashed the check anyway."

"Who can get there? The Air Force has people in California. Maybe the new Navy command, but Buffy hasn't met them yet. O'Neil could fly from Colorado if we call Hammond."

"I'm going."

Xander waited for the rest of the sentence.

It did not arrive.

"You mean you're calling."

"I mean I'm going to California."

"No."

Bartlet raised his eyebrows.

"Respectfully," Xander added. "Very respectfully. Presidentially respectfully. Still no."

"You came here asking for presidential confirmation."

"I was thinking of the voice kind. The kind that comes over a phone. From inside the building designed to keep you from getting shot."

"Buffy is already telling her."

"Then use the phone. Or a military plane. Tell the Secret Service. Tell Leo. Tell literally anyone whose job includes continuity of government."

"A military flight takes hours."

"The President disappearing out a window takes years off people's lives."

"I won't be disappearing. You'll know where I am."

"I am not the chain of command. I'm not even old enough to vote for the chain of command."

Bartlet took his coat from the stand.

"Sir, presidential consent does not make Superman authorized presidential transportation."

"A sitting President cannot abduct himself."

"Leo is going to have a competing legal theory."

"Leo frequently does."

"There are succession rules. Emergency communications. Nuclear command authority. Airspace. A protection detail that will use words about me which I am too young to hear."

"You listened in the briefings."

"This is not the moment to be proud of me."

Bartlet crossed to the window.

Xander moved in front of it. "At minimum, tell Leo."

"If I tell Leo, Leo tells the detail, the detail tells the Air Force, the Air Force develops a route, California closes roads, and Joyce Summers learns the truth at midnight after six federal agencies have held a scheduling conference."

"That is how not losing the President works."

"Mrs. Landingham," Bartlet called.

She appeared in the doorway.

"The President wants to leave through the window," Xander said.

Mrs. Landingham looked at Bartlet. "Will you be back for the residence call?"

"I expect so."

"Then take your heavier coat."

Xander stared at her.

"You too?"

"No, dear. I think this is a terrible idea. But he has already decided, and California is cold after dark."

Bartlet exchanged coats. On a sheet of presidential stationery he wrote: Leo, urgent family matter in California. With Superman. Back soon. J.

He placed it on the Resolute desk.

"That note will not materially improve anything," Xander said.

"It's informative."

"It's a confession."

Bartlet opened the Oval window.

Xander held a hand against the frame, listening with senses he barely understood. "Three seconds after the guard passes the west colonnade. That's our window. The alarm sensor has a pressure differential—I can shield it, but we have to be moving.

"Mr. President."

"Mr. Harris. A girl who has carried an adult failure for two years is already walking into her house. We can spend tonight obtaining permission to acknowledge it, or we can arrive while acknowledgment matters."

Xander had spent his whole life deferring to men who sounded that sure of themselves, and this man was also the President of the United States.

"Leo is going to kill me."

"He'll have to stand in line."

Xander wrapped one arm securely around Bartlet, used the cape to shield him from the first rush of air, and stepped onto the exterior ledge.

Behind them, Mrs. Landingham checked the note and closed the office door.

The protection detail registered Superman's departure on their perimeter sensors.

It took another forty-seven seconds to understand that the President had gone with him.

* * *

Joyce was sorting gallery invoices at the kitchen counter when Buffy came home.

She looked first at the clock, then at Giles behind her daughter, then at the tear in Buffy's coat.

"What happened?"

Buffy closed the door.

Every softer opening disappeared. She could ask about dinner. She could say they needed to talk and spend an hour avoiding what about. She could go upstairs, change the coat, cover the bruise, and let one more ordinary evening build itself over the truth.

"Vampires are real," she said. "Demons are real. I'm the Slayer. I fight them. That's where I go at night and why I get hurt and why everything happened at Hemery."

Joyce stared at her.

"Buffy."

"I know how it sounds."

"Do you?"

"Yes. I told you before."

Giles remained near the doorway, saying nothing yet.

Joyce set down the invoice. "Is this because of what happened in Los Angeles? Because if you're having the same thoughts again, we can call Dr. Lerner. We can get help without making it into another crisis."

Buffy's face tightened. "Those weren't thoughts. I was telling the truth."

"You were fifteen and terrified. You had burned down part of your school. Your father and I did what the doctors told us."

"You put me somewhere I couldn't leave because I told you vampires were real."

"Because you believed they were."

"They are."

Joyce looked at Giles. "Did you tell her to do this?"

"No."

"But you believe it."

"I know it to be true."

"You're the school librarian."

"I am also Buffy's Watcher."

The word sat wrong in her mouth, stranger and more specific than she'd expected.

Joyce came around the counter. "What does that mean?"

Buffy answered before Giles could. "It means he trains me. Researches what I fight. The Watchers Council sent him when we moved here."

"A council sent an adult man into my daughter's school to train her to fight imaginary monsters."

"The monsters aren't imaginary."

"Buffy, listen to yourself."

"I've been listening to myself alone for two years."

Joyce took a step back, her chin pulling in toward her chest.

Buffy did not take it back.

"The cuts, the torn clothes, sneaking out, getting expelled. I wasn't in gangs. I wasn't picking fights because I wanted attention. There were vampires at Hemery. I burned the gym because people were trapped inside."

"There were police reports."

"The police don't write vampire. They write gangs on PCP or animal attack or barbecue-fork accident. Sunnydale does it all the time."

"And Mr. Giles has an explanation for every piece of evidence that doesn't fit."

"Yes," Giles said. "That is one reason you should not be expected to accept this solely on my word."

Joyce turned on him. "You don't get points for sounding reasonable. How long have you known?"

"Since before Buffy enrolled at Sunnydale High."

"You knew what she was doing every night."

"Yes."

"You let her?"

Giles absorbed the question. "I trained her for a duty imposed without her consent. I believed secrecy protected her identity and her family. Whatever the Council taught me about necessity, I should have confronted what that secrecy required of Buffy and what it denied you. I did not."

"Duty," Joyce repeated. "She's sixteen."

"I know."

"Do you?"

"Not soon enough."

Buffy moved to the dining table. "Mom, look at me."

"I am looking at you."

"No. You're looking for the explanation where I'm sick and Giles is lying because that world makes sense. I need you to actually look."

She placed one hand beneath the solid oak edge and lifted.

The entire table rose from the floor.

Invoices slid. A ceramic bowl tipped from the center. Joyce reached too late.

Buffy's other hand caught the bowl before it fell six inches.

She held the table level, set the bowl back in place, and lowered both without a sound.

Joyce took one step backward.

"How did you do that?"

"Slayer. Strength, speed, healing. Dreams sometimes. A really bad social life."

"That isn't possible."

"I know."

"Do it again."

Buffy lifted the table again.

Joyce stared at her daughter's narrow arm, at the wood held steady by one hand, at Giles standing too quietly in her kitchen.

"Some kind of trick," she said, but her voice came out thinner than she intended.

Buffy set the table down. "Darla. The woman you invited inside last year. She was a vampire. She came because of me. She could enter because you invited her. I never told you what the rule was, so you couldn't know."

Joyce's face went white. "She drank coffee in this house."

"Yes."

"You told me she was a tutor."

"Because tutor was easier than vampire after the last time I told the truth."

Joyce looked toward the living room as if Darla might still be there.

"What else has been in my house?"

"Nothing I know about."

"That you know about."

"Yes."

She couldn't quite make herself believe it, even now that she had no reason not to.

Joyce demanded names. Incidents. Proof. Buffy told her about the Master, the Harvest, the things which had worn familiar faces. Giles explained vampires without turning the answer into a lecture. He described the Council and did not defend its absence. Each fact made the next less impossible and the whole more unbearable.

"You've been doing this every night?" Joyce asked.

"Most nights."

"And going to school."

"Usually."

"When you're hurt?"

"Giles patches me up. Willow. Xander. I heal fast."

"You let children treat her injuries?" Joyce asked Giles.

"Yes."

"Why wasn't she in a hospital?"

"Because the injuries would have required explanations which risked exposure."

"Exposure to whom? I am her mother."

"Yes."

There was no defense in Giles's answer. Joyce found that more frightening than an argument.

"Who else knows?"

"Willow, Xander, Angel, Ms. Calendar before she left," Buffy said. "Some others know pieces."

"Everyone except me."

"Not everyone."

"Everyone you trusted."

Buffy flinched.

"Mom."

"No. You said you told us. You told me."

"I tried."

The memory changed shape in Joyce's face.

Buffy at fifteen, shaking and hoarse, insisting there had been men with dead faces. Hank closing the curtains while Joyce called the number the therapist provided. The admission desk. Buffy's hand slipping from hers when an orderly said parents could not accompany her past the locked door. Every professional explanation about fixation, trauma, and dangerous fantasy.

Buffy had begged her to believe.

Joyce had believed the people who locked the door.

"Oh, God," she said.

Buffy stood absolutely still.

"You were telling the truth."

"Yes."

"And we put you in that place."

Buffy did not rescue her from the sentence.

"Yes."

Joyce covered her mouth. Horror arrived without the mercy of ignorance. It reached backward through every fight after Los Angeles, every accusation of lying, every punishment for an absence, every ruined shirt Joyce had treated as evidence that Buffy did not care what her behavior cost.

"I thought we were helping you."

"I know."

"Why didn't you try again?"

Buffy's eyes filled, but her voice stayed level. "Because I learned what happened when I did."

The knock at the front door came into the silence.

Nobody moved.

It came again, ordinary and polite.

Giles crossed the living room and checked through the curtain. Then he turned back wearing an expression Buffy had never seen on him.

"Who is it?" Joyce asked.

"The President of the United States."

Joyce stared at him. "This is not the time for another impossible thing."

"I'm afraid it appears to be."

Giles opened the door.

Bartlet stood on the porch beside Superman.

"Mrs. Summers?" Bartlet said. "I'm Josiah Bartlet. May we come in?"

Joyce looked at the familiar face from every evening broadcast, then at the red cape beside him, then at Buffy.

"You called the President?"

"I didn't," Buffy said.

"I did," Xander said. "Sort of. I asked for a phone call. Things got a little bigger than a phone call."

Bartlet stepped inside only after Joyce moved back. Xander closed the door behind them.

"Mrs. Summers, your daughter has already told you the important truth. I am not here to reveal it, and I'm not here to tell you what the Watchers Council says you should believe."

"Then why are you here?"

"To tell you what the United States government has independently verified. Vampires, demons, and other things you were told were nightmares exist. Buffy possesses physical abilities which are real and measurable. We have confirmed that she has used those abilities repeatedly to defend lives. The danger she described is real. So is the work."

Joyce gripped the back of a chair.

"The government knows?"

"It does now. Not soon enough."

"Are you taking her?"

"No."

"Recruiting her?"

"No. We are building a support command because Buffy has been performing necessary dangerous work without protected medicine, transport, equipment, compensation, or dependable backup. It exists to support her choices, not replace them."

Joyce looked at Buffy. "Compensation?"

"Hazard pay," Buffy said, and the words sounded even stranger in her own kitchen.

"She's a child."

"Yes," Bartlet said. "That is why every adult institution involved should be ashamed it took this long to ask who was supporting her."

Giles lowered his eyes.

"Why didn't anyone tell me?" Joyce demanded.

Bartlet did not look at Buffy before answering. "I can tell you why the federal government did not. Until recently, we did not possess the facts. Mr. Giles can answer for the Council. Buffy can tell you why she made the choices she made. I will not use my office to answer either question for them."

"But you believe her."

"Yes."

The last refuge collapsed.

Joyce looked at her daughter and saw the locked clinic door again.

"I did that to you."

Buffy swallowed. "You didn't know."

"You told me."

"Yes."

"I should have believed you."

Buffy said nothing.

Bartlet moved away from the center of the room. He had crossed a continent to establish facts. He did not turn that authority into permission to mediate the grief those facts created.

"Mrs. Summers," he said, "you will have a secure number, medical contacts, and people who can answer questions after tonight. You will be included in emergency planning. But none of that requires Buffy to make you feel better about what happened before."

Joyce's face crumpled and steadied in the same breath.

"No," she said. "It doesn't."

The President of the United States stood in her living room. Superman remained near the front door. None of it reassured her.

It made denial impossible.

* * *

The Secret Service discovered the empty Oval Office in stages.

An agent entered through the west door to confirm the President was ready for the economic briefing. He found an open window, a missing coat, and no President.

He checked the study.

He checked the private dining room.

He read the note.

The next thirty seconds used very few words and activated nearly every secure telephone in the building.

Leo arrived at a run. The Special Agent in Charge handed him the note without comment.

Leo read it once.

"No."

He read it again, as if a second pass might cause a President he had known for decades to become a different man.

"Superman was here," the agent said.

"I gathered that from the part where he wrote 'with Superman.'"

"Mrs. Landingham saw him arrive."

She stood in the doorway, composed amid a building accelerating toward panic.

"Xander came to speak to the President about an urgent family matter," she said. "Nobody was under immediate attack. I placed him in the adjoining room. The President went in."

"Did you see them leave?" the agent asked.

"No."

"Did the President state a destination?"

"California."

Leo closed his eyes. "Sunnydale."

The agent spoke into his radio. California was a state, not a destination. The detail had no call sign to track, no flight plan, no transponder, no motorcade, no advance team, and no communications link to the protectee. Radar contacts moving at Superman's reported speed produced more possibilities than answers.

"Altitude?" someone asked.

"Unknown."

"Route?"

"Unknown."

"Estimated arrival?"

Leo looked at the open window. "Probably before we finish asking."

Fitzwallace reached the Oval four minutes later. He heard the summary and called Coronado before anyone suggested a slower option.

Chegwidden answered from the planning room.

"A.J., the Commander in Chief has entered Sunnydale."

Chegwidden looked at O'Neil and Urgayle. "Under whose protection?"

"Superman's. Unscheduled departure from the Oval. No detail."

"You lost the President?"

"The President lost us."

"Destination?"

"Summers residence."

Chegwidden covered the receiver. "The mission has advanced."

Urgayle picked up the vehicle keys.

O'Neil asked, "Does Summers know?"

"I doubt anyone knows."

"Then we secure after the fact and keep the neighborhood from noticing a federal invasion."

Chegwidden returned to the call. "My people are moving. Send Secret Service recognition, current threat information, and a direct contact number. Keep every agency without the supernatural compartment out of Sunnydale unless the detail specifically requires it."

"Agreed."

"And Fitz?"

"Yes?"

"When this is over, explain to the President that my command was not scheduled to acquire a presidential-protection mission before it acquired a name."

"You can tell him yourself."

O'Neil and Urgayle left Coronado with two cleared security specialists in an unmarked Suburban. The Secret Service wanted lights, air support, and every law-enforcement agency between San Diego and Sunnydale. Urgayle wanted none of them announcing the route to City Hall.

They compromised on speed and discretion. A California Highway Patrol contact cleared problems ahead without creating a visible escort. An Air Force helicopter moved a presidential detail toward a landing zone outside town. The Suburban reached Revello Drive first.

Urgayle looked once at the quiet houses, parked cars, porch lights, and dog walker approaching from the east.

"Worst possible place for a perimeter," he said.

"Because it's residential?" O'Neil asked.

"Because everybody knows what belongs. Four black vehicles and men talking into their sleeves become the only thing anyone sees."

He sent one specialist to the end of the block in ordinary clothes and placed the other where the back yard and alley could be watched without standing under a streetlight. The Suburban parked two streets away. Urgayle walked the perimeter himself, noting windows, approaches, rooflines, and the complete absence of positions he trusted.

O'Neil approached the front door alone.

Inside, Joyce had reached the question about whether Buffy could be killed.

Nobody tried to soften the answer.

"Yes," Buffy said.

Joyce gripped the chair harder. "How many times have you almost died?"

"That's not a useful number."

"It is to me."

"Mom, tonight can't hold every night."

Bartlet said, "She's right. You need an emergency plan first. The history will still be terrible tomorrow."

Joyce looked at him. "You say that as a President?"

"As a parent who has learned that fear likes to disguise itself as a demand for complete information."

The knock came.

Xander looked through the door. "Military. One person."

"That sentence has become less reassuring since China," Buffy said.

He checked again. "O'Neil. One L. Different person."

Buffy opened the door.

Lieutenant O'Neil looked past her to Bartlet.

"Mr. President. The Secret Service asked me to explain that consenting to your own abduction from the Oval Office does not make it an authorized movement."

"I wasn't abducted."

"No, sir. We understand you insisted."

"I left a note."

"Yes, sir. They found it beside the open window. It has not materially improved morale."

Xander looked at Bartlet. "I said that."

"You were right too. Don't become difficult about it."

O'Neil turned to Joyce and Buffy. "Mrs. Summers, Buffy. I'm Jordan O'Neil. Admiral Chegwidden assigned me to lead the support element. I apologize that our first contact is taking place during a presidential recovery. No one will enter your home without permission."

"How many people are outside?" Joyce asked.

"Four from my command within the neighborhood. A Secret Service detail is approaching. Command posts and aircraft will remain outside town where possible."

"Why?"

"Because the President came here without telling them."

Joyce looked at Bartlet. "Does this happen often?"

"Not in precisely this form."

"That's not an answer," Leo said through the secure telephone O'Neil had just handed him.

Bartlet accepted it. "Leo."

"Sir."

No.

"I left a note."

"The note has been reviewed by the Secret Service, the military office, the communications agency, White House counsel, and one physician who was apparently concerned I'd swallowed it. Nobody found a protection detail in it."

"I'm safe."

"You are in a house above an active Hellmouth."

"It's a very nice house."

Joyce made a choked, breathy sound that was almost a laugh.

"The detail will be there in eight minutes," Leo said. "You will cooperate with the return plan."

"Yes, Leo."

"Put Lieutenant O'Neil back on."

O'Neil took the telephone and moved to the hall. She commanded the government's response without once attempting to command the room.

Outside, Urgayle intercepted the arriving Secret Service lead and converted urgency into positions. Agents entered only after Joyce consented. Vehicles remained dispersed. The landing zone stayed outside Sunnydale. City police received a bland federal-protection notification which omitted the protectee's identity.

Backward, improvised, and late, a perimeter came into existence.

Inside, Bartlet gave Joyce the secure contact number and O'Neil described what the command could provide immediately. Joyce asked whether Buffy would be followed. O'Neil said no. She asked whether the government would take blood. O'Neil said only what treatment required, with consent. She asked whether refusing a mission would end the medical protection or pay.

"No," O'Neil said. "And if anyone tells you otherwise, you call me or Admiral Chegwidden."

Buffy watched her mother absorb the fact that there were now adults she could challenge by name.

Bartlet put on the heavier coat Mrs. Landingham had selected.

"I can't repair what tonight uncovered," he told Joyce. "I can make sure the government does not repeat the pattern by asking Buffy to carry our responsibilities too."

Joyce nodded, unable to thank him and unwilling to pretend gratitude was the largest feeling in the room.

He accepted that.

At the door, Bartlet looked at Buffy. "You did the hard part before I arrived. Remember that."

"Hard to forget."

The Secret Service moved him into a vehicle capable of producing a return trip everyone could track. Xander was not invited to provide transportation.

"Am I under arrest?" he asked O'Neil.

"No."

"Does Leo know that?"

"He has several theories."

O'Neil glanced toward the living room before lowering her voice. "For the record, tonight's entry was emergency presidential protection, not activation of the support mission. We still owe Buffy a formal brief and her decision before the advance element deploys."

"You came into town before telling her."

"The President arrived before the command knew where he was. I will document the exception and Admiral Chegwidden will review it. If Buffy says tonight proves she doesn't trust us, we live with that and rebuild trust."

Xander considered her. "You really put that stuff in reports?"

"Especially the embarrassing parts. Those are the parts future officers most need someone to have admitted."

Buffy joined them near the door. "Temporary perimeter tonight. Formal briefing before you come back for anything that isn't hauling away another runaway President."

"Agreed," O'Neil said.

"No watching Mom after tonight unless I ask or you have a specific threat."

"Agreed."

"And the people outside know Angel might show up?"

"They have instructions not to treat nonhuman identification as hostile intent."

"Instructions aren't judgment."

"No. That's why Urgayle removed half the original roster."

Buffy looked through the window at the quiet block. "Tomorrow. You, me, Giles, and the admiral by secure line. You explain everything before anybody brings equipment."

"Yes."

The first operational agreement of the support command was not about an operation. It was permission to leave and terms for coming back.

Urgayle waited until the presidential vehicles had cleared the block, then reduced the perimeter as quietly as he had built it.

Buffy closed the door.

She and Joyce faced each other across a living room which looked exactly as it had that morning.

Neither could go back to not knowing what they now knew.
