And, more to the point, the observers are not ornamental. They are credentialed, and they know what they’re looking at. If we tried to freeze them out, we’d be announcing to the world that the U.S. government is going to unilaterally break the Treaty. That’s not an option, not for us, and not for you.”

A pause. On the NATO split-screen, one of the advisers scribbled something on a legal pad, then passed it along the row.

Bartlet looked at Leo, then at the camera. “That brings me to item two.”

He closed the first folder and opened a red-bordered one, each page stamped “EXSUM” in bold at the top. “Since 1998, there has been a compartmentalized operation at Cheyenne Mountain, formerly known as Stargate Command, under the direct authority of the Joint Chiefs and the Office of the President. The program’s existence was classified Tier One. Its function—” here he let the weight build “—was to manage, operate, and exploit an ancient ring-based technology found in Egypt in the late 1920s. The Stargate.”

Bartlet watched the British adviser’s face and saw, in real time, the fifty years of poker face falter.

“The program has conducted over one hundred off-world missions. There have been conflicts. There have been losses. But for the last three years, the greatest threat has been domestic—not foreign. There are elements within our own government who view the technology as a means to a unilateral world order. I want it stated, on this record and for all time, that is not my intention, nor the policy of this administration.”

Bernard, quieter: “Why now?”

“Because,” said Bartlet, “the incident in Antarctica is going to break the compartment. The same people who want to control the Stargate are going to push for a lockdown, and they’ll succeed unless we get out ahead of them. That means bringing our partners into the loop. Not just as witnesses, but as actors.”

The room on the other end of the video feed went quiet. Not the diplomatic hush of a room waiting its turn, but the absolute, shared silence of people absorbing a paradigm shift.

Bartlet let it sit. He’d learned, from years at the front of a classroom, that silence did more to teach than any two-page speech.

Leo took over the baton. “We have prepared briefing materials for your offices. They’re coded and set to self-destruct in twenty-four hours. After you’ve had time to review, we’ll set a second call with your technical experts and ours. You will have access to the unredacted mission logs, and to all physical artifacts under joint custody.”

De Keyser: “You expect us to believe—”

Bartlet cut in, not unkindly: “I expect you to check the math. The logs are all there. The crews—many of whom were recruited from allied countries—are named and accounted for. And we are prepared to receive your own people at Cheyenne Mountain to observe current operations.”

C.J. interjected from the door, voice steady: “The press will get wind of the Antarctic dig within the week. I can spin the cover story for now, but once foreign scientists are on site, the temperature will rise. Our objective is to keep things at a simmer until we have a common position.”

Bernard: “And the Enterprise?”

“Still on ice,” said Bartlet. “It will remain so until we jointly decide on next steps. But I will not allow a repeat of the Cold War race for the spoils.”

On the Belgian side, the legal pad scribbler whispered something to a woman in glasses, who nodded and leaned into the mic: “President Bartlet, if I may—how many people in the United States government currently know about the Stargate program?”

Bartlet didn’t hesitate. “Fewer than two hundred, including technical and security support.”

The woman: “So, you have operated unilaterally for the entire length of the program.”

Bartlet: “That is correct.”

Bernard: “How can we trust you now?”

Bartlet laced his fingers and stared into the camera, letting the tired in his face show. “Because I’m telling you now, when I could just as easily have told you nothing. Because we have already put observers on the ground. Because I trust the future of this world more than I trust the future of my own country, and if that gets me voted out, then I’ll take the blame. But I will not sleepwalk into a planetary incident because we were too proud to admit what we found.”

There was a long, long silence.

Zoey watched her father from the back, the way you watch a building catch fire. He didn’t look at her, but he could sense her there, a fixed point in the gravitational math of his universe.

On the screen, Bernard pinched the bridge of his nose, then composed himself. “Thank you, Mr. President. We will review your materials and set the next call as soon as possible.”

“Thank you, Sir Bernard,” said Bartlet, and meant it.

The call ended, replaced by the blue-on-black of the White House secure desktop.

C.J. let out a breath, then pushed off the door and walked toward the table, where she took up the mug of cold coffee and sniffed it.

“Should I make a fresh pot?” she asked, but it wasn’t really a question.

Leo said, “You just detonated a secret that cost three administrations their sleep. You want the first quote tomorrow, or do we push the press until the Hill has their heads on straight?”

Bartlet didn’t answer. He stared at the mug, then at Zoey, who finally set down her legal pad.

She said, “You did good, Dad.” It was quiet, but not shy.

He almost smiled. “Good and enough, or just enough?”

She shrugged. “Enough that I don’t hate you for keeping it secret. Enough that I’d want to know before the aliens showed up on TV.”

He nodded, once.

C.J. set down the mug and walked out, making a note on her pad as she left.

Leo started to follow, but then paused. “Sir, when the British come back tomorrow, they’ll want to know if the survivors from the ship are going to be treated as persons or as artifacts. You want to draw that line now, or after the legal teams have at it?”

Bartlet rubbed his temple. “Let’s wait. Sometimes the lawyers can surprise you.”

Leo grunted, then headed out, flipping the tab to page eight as he moved.

Zoey lingered. She walked to the head of the table, took the mug, and went to the sideboard for fresh coffee. She poured it herself, brought it back, and set it at her father’s right hand.

“You really sure you want this to be your legacy?” she asked.

He looked at the mug, then at her. “I want my legacy to be that I left things a little bit less screwed up than I found them. That’s enough for me.”

She didn’t argue. She took her legal pad and left the Situation Room, her silhouette clean against the bright hallway outside.

Bartlet sat alone for a moment, the hum of the conference equipment already fading.

Then, with the practiced grace of a man who had done this a thousand times, he straightened his tie, took a sip of the coffee, and toggled the relay for the next meeting.

The future was here, and it wasn’t going to wait.

Scene 2

The Summers kitchen was the one room in the house that looked best in the morning. Yellow light came through the slider, warming the old round table and catching on the Navy-blue seal of the folder between Buffy and her mother. The refrigerator behind Joyce was still a mess of school photographs, vacation magnets, and an evolving grocery list. Today’s list was topped by “eggs” in Sharpie and, beneath it in Buffy’s handwriting, “Jell-O, the good kind.”

This was not the conversation they had the night President Bartlet left through the front door and took the last possible excuse for disbelief with him. They had already done vampires, invitation rules, the Council, patrol injuries, emergency numbers, and the location of every stake in the house. This was the conversation Joyce had demanded that night: records, not prophecy. The Navy had finally delivered them.

Joyce rested both hands on the folder. She had read it twice. The first pass had been as Buffy’s mother. The second had been with a legal pad beside her and every euphemism underlined.

“Essential personnel,” she said.

Buffy looked at the phrase upside down. “They like labels. It makes the impossible fit in a filing cabinet.”

“Critical path. Primary responder. Irreplaceable local capability.” Joyce tapped each line with one finger. “Those are not labels. Those are ways of saying they have built a plan around my daughter.”

“They built support around what I was already doing,” Buffy said. “There’s a difference.”

Joyce held her gaze. “Is there?”

Buffy considered the folder instead of answering quickly. That, more than any reassurance, told Joyce the question was being taken seriously.

“Chegwidden put the difference in writing,” Buffy said at last. “I can refuse a mission. I can refuse equipment. I can tell them to leave. The emergency clause is for people already in danger, not for sending me somewhere because Washington wants a Slayer on the scene.”

Joyce opened to the marked page. The refusal language was there, boxed in blue pencil and cross-referenced three times. Giles had added a note in the margin. O’Neil had initialed beneath it. Someone from JAG had attached a page explaining exactly how a sixteen-year-old could withdraw consent from a classified support arrangement.

“And if they decide the emergency clause means whatever they need it to mean?” Joyce asked.

“Then I call you, Giles, and the Admiral, in that order. If nobody listens, I call the President. Apparently he accepts complaints by air delivery.”

Joyce tried not to smile. She failed briefly. “We are not making that a standard procedure.”

“It wasn’t standard the first time.”

Joyce turned to the casualty summary. Unlike the Council’s histories, it treated Buffy as a person who could bleed. Dates, injuries, response times, treatment received, treatment unavailable. The count at the bottom was worse for being typed neatly.

“You told me you had nearly died,” Joyce said. “You did not tell me anyone had started counting.”

“I started counting. Then Giles did. The Navy just made a spreadsheet.” Buffy touched one entry. “Some of these are arguable. They call the Master a clinical death. I call it getting back up.”

“I am not comforted by the accounting dispute.”

“Wasn’t really going for comfort.”

Joyce read the next page more slowly. It listed the things that had changed since their first night of planning: a medical contact who would answer without questions, a vehicle close enough to matter, a protected telephone line, and people under orders to preserve evidence instead of explaining it away. None of it made patrol safe. It did make the aftermath less lonely.

“This part I understand,” she said. “A doctor. Transportation. Someone answering the telephone. Those are things you should have had two years ago.”

Buffy’s shoulders eased. “Yeah.”

The incident map came next. Joyce knew what the dots meant now. She recognized the school, Restfield, the Bronze, the hospital, and the ugly concentration around streets she drove every week. One blue circle covered Revello Drive without touching the house itself.

“Support radius,” Buffy said before Joyce could ask. “Not a target marker. Urgayle showed me the notation.”

“I know. He explained it when the car changed positions Tuesday.” Joyce turned the page. “I am learning their habits.”

“He’s probably terrified of you.”

“Good.”

Joyce reached the personnel appendix. Giles’s entry was dense with qualifications and carefully neutral language about the Council. Willow had corrected two technical descriptions in red ink. Xander’s page had a handwritten objection to the phrase “secondary field asset” and, beneath it, a different hand noting that the objection was sustained. Ethan’s name appeared throughout the working notes rather than in a separate box, as if nobody had found a safe bureaucratic category for him.

“They finally managed to describe your friends without pretending they appeared last week,” Joyce said.

Buffy leaned over to inspect the amendments. “Willow made them redo half of it. Giles made them redo the other half. Ethan objected to being documented on philosophical grounds, then submitted six pages of corrections.”

“And Xander?”

“Drew a cape on his photograph and claimed he had no further notes.”

Joyce closed the appendix. “That sounds like him.”

For a moment the kitchen was quiet except for the refrigerator motor and the faint scrape of a branch against the window.

“What do you need from me now?” Joyce asked.

Buffy did not say just keep being you. That answer had belonged to the first conversation, when both of them were trying to make knowledge small enough to survive the night.

“Learn the phone tree,” she said. “If I miss check-in, call the local number before you call the police. If the wards change color, don’t touch them. If I come home hurt and say I’m fine, make me sit down anyway. And if somebody with credentials says I agreed to something I didn’t agree to, ask for it in writing.”

Joyce nodded once. “I can do that.”

“I know.”

“And Admiral Chegwidden still owes me the meeting I requested.”

“Friday at four. O’Neil set it up yesterday.”

Joyce reopened the folder to the page of questions she had written herself. “Good. He can explain why the medical authorization requires three signatures and why none of them are mine.”

Buffy winced in advance on the Admiral’s behalf. “Maybe start with hello.”

“I will consider it.”

Buffy reached for the coffee she had forgotten to drink. It had gone cold. She drank it anyway.

Joyce looked once more at the folder, no longer as proof of a hidden life but as an imperfect plan for the life they were now facing together.

“You are still going to school today,” she said.

Buffy blinked. “That’s your takeaway?”

“Normal is harder to keep than weird, apparently. Someone very stubborn told me that.”

Buffy smiled and stood. “She sounds wise.”

“She has her moments.”

Outside, the day had gotten brighter. Inside, the folder remained open on the kitchen table, its margins crowded with the evidence that disclosure was not a single confession. It was maintenance.

Scene 3

The Harris living room was always darkest just after sunset. Some architectural flaw in the subdivision’s layout meant the porch light outside was useless, and the low ceiling trapped the dusk in a way that made the couch look even more sunken than it was. Xander walked in and, out of habit, stepped around the coffee table at the far end—one leg shorter than the others, so the whole thing tipped if you so much as breathed near it.

The TV played a cable news channel with the sound set to “submarine.” Tony Harris sat in the recliner, remote balanced on his chest, a can of Coors wedged between two fingers. The can was half empty, which meant this was probably the third or fourth of the night, but the way Tony’s head snapped up as Xander entered suggested the buzz had plateaued. Jessica Harris was on the couch, glass of white wine in her hand, her feet tucked up and her eyes fixed on some point just above the TV. The wine bottle—“Franzia,” its box squatting behind a stack of old TV Guides on the counter—was at least half-gone.

Xander slid onto the couch as close to the door as possible. The satchel with the uniform stayed between his feet, close enough that he could toe it if things went sideways.

He didn’t wait for permission. He said, “I have to talk to you both. It’s not bad, but it’s not nothing.”

Tony muted the TV, but didn’t look away. “You got a girl in trouble?”

Jessica rolled her eyes. “Tony, not everyone is you.”

Xander grinned, small and sharp, then let it fall. “No girls, no trouble. Just… There’s a government thing. I have to tell you so you don’t freak if it comes up.”

Jessica blinked, then set down the wine. “What kind of thing? Like a draft? Is there a war?”

“No war. No draft.” Xander glanced at the coffee table, then said, “Just an arrangement. I helped out with something in D.C., and now they… have a way to reach me if something comes up. But it’s not a job, and it’s not full-time. It doesn’t change anything here.”

Tony made a face, half-skeptical, half-interested. “You get paid?”

“No.”

Jessica frowned, worried now. “Is this—what, like a witness thing? Do you need a lawyer?”

“It’s not criminal. I’m not testifying. It’s just—” He hesitated. “I’m on a list. If there’s an emergency, or if they need something, they call. That’s it.”

Tony sat forward, can resting on his thigh. “You got a name, a card, something to prove this isn’t a load of—”

“Ask Mr. Giles,” Xander said. “Ask Principal Snyder, or—” He stopped. “Ask the Vice Principal. He has a contact. You can call and verify it.”

Jessica: “So you’re not leaving school?”

Xander: “Nope. Not unless it’s an emergency.”

Tony: “What kind of emergency?”

Xander shrugged. “The kind where they call, and I decide if I go.”

The silence stretched. On the TV, a B-roll of a traffic jam cycled in endless loop. Tony scratched his chin, then said, “You’re not getting paid.”

Xander: “No.”

Tony: “So what’s the point?”

Xander looked at him, then at Jessica, then at the floor. “The point is, I’m still here. I’m not running away. I’m not going to ditch the house or the school or anything. But I needed to tell you so it didn’t blindside you.”

Tony’s face twisted, then flattened. “This is one of your jokes, isn’t it.”

Xander, flat: “No joke.”

Jessica picked up the wine again, sipped. “Are you in danger?”

Xander shook his head, then, more honestly, said, “I don’t know.”

She nodded, as if she’d expected that. “So we just… go on like always?”

“Yeah,” he said, a little softer than intended.

Tony: “And if someone comes to the door—FBI or whatever—I tell them what?”

Xander: “You tell them I’m not here. Unless it’s a real emergency. Then you can call the number in the kitchen. It’s on the fridge, blue sticky note.”

Tony grunted, then sat back. “If you get paid, you tell me.”

Xander said, “Sure.”

The silence returned, but it wasn’t hostile. It was just… full.

After a minute, Jessica said, “You hungry?”

Xander shook his head. “I’m good.”

He stood, grabbed the satchel, and let himself out. No door slam. Just a clean, quiet exit.

He sat on the front step for a few seconds, letting the night settle. The neighborhood was quiet: a dog barking three streets over, the faint chemical drift of a backyard barbecue, the rattle of a sprinkler somewhere up the block. The houses all looked the same, their lights yellow and warm, and for a moment, Xander let himself believe he could just walk away, disappear into any of them, and no one would care.

He checked the watch. It was synced, green, steady.

Xander stood and started walking, hands in his pockets, the sidewalk cool under his feet, the normalness of it all a little too perfect to trust.

Behind him, the Harris house was dark. Ahead, the world waited.

He kept moving.

Scene 4

The school library after hours felt like the eye of a hurricane: the world outside spun fast and loud, but inside the stacks, everything was padded in books and filtered light. It was Giles’s favorite time, when the students were gone and the only sounds were the hum of the AC and the slow tick of the wall clock above the check-out desk.

Today, the central table was more crowded than usual. Four chairs, four occupants: Buffy, at the head, legs stretched out and boots braced on the edge; Willow, to her left, laptop open, highlighter in one hand; Giles, beside her, posture perfect, blue pen hovering as if every sentence needed annotating. Xander brought up the rear, slouched but alert, a can of soda balanced on the arm of his chair.

A secure laptop, black and boxy, sat at the far end of the table, its camera LED winking red. On the screen, Admiral Chegwidden appeared in high-res grayscale, with a JAG officer two feet behind and a USN flag arrayed over his right shoulder.

The paperwork in front of them was an inch thick, but Willow had collated the key docs into four identical packets, each stapled and stamped “UNCLASSIFIED UPON SIGNATURE.” The cover page: “Memorandum of Understanding / Joint Operational Framework / SDF-01 Task Element.” In smaller print: “Buffy Anne Summers, Lead; Giles Rupert, Civilian Coordinator; Rosenberg Willow, Technical Liaison; Harris Alexander, Field Volunteer (Independent).”

Giles started: “The protocols appear comprehensive. I do have some questions about the medical evacuation clause—”

Chegwidden cut him off, politely but absolutely. “The clause is there to ensure field assets are never left exposed. If you signal for extraction, you get it, no questions. It’s not a lever for command and control, it’s a shield. If you wish, I can walk you through the escalation tree.”

Giles, mollified, tapped the pen and nodded. “That will suffice.”

Buffy leafed through her copy, skipping to the signature page. “So, if we sign, that’s it? We’re a task force, like, Navy-official?”

Chegwidden: “You’re a voluntary element, under civilian direction. The Navy provides support—intel, logistics, medevac, airlift—but you set your own rules of engagement. Nobody’s going to order you anywhere.”

Xander grinned. “Not even for pizza runs?”

Chegwidden didn’t blink. “Mr. Harris, in the event of a pizza emergency, you’ll have the full backing of Naval Aviation.”

Giles allowed himself the faintest of smiles. Willow actually giggled.

Chegwidden: “Now, if you’ll initial section four, page nine, and sign the last page, we’ll have our agreement.”

Buffy flipped to page nine, scrawled a quick BA SUMMERS, and passed the packet to Giles, who signed with old-world flourish. Willow took extra time, reading every line, highlighting one phrase—“critical civilian input”—then signed in neat, looping script.

Xander got the packet last. He read the relevant paragraph, then looked up at the screen. “Just so we’re clear—this doesn’t mean I take orders.”

Chegwidden: “Mr. Harris, if I wanted someone who took orders, I would have drafted you.”

Xander signed, then leaned back, clearly pleased.

Willow scanned each signed page, uploaded the PDFs to the secure portal, and sent the originals via the overnight envelope provided by the Navy courier that afternoon. The JAG officer on the other end clicked a button and the screen registered: “RECEIPT ACKNOWLEDGED.”

Chegwidden looked at each of them in turn, then said, “Thank you, all. You’ve done more than anyone could ask. You’ll have the support you need. And the autonomy.”

Giles: “We appreciate the trust, Admiral.”

Buffy: “And the helicopters.”

Chegwidden smiled, dry and brief. “Don’t crash any. They’re expensive.”

The screen faded to the Navy seal, then to black.

The four sat in silence. Giles removed his glasses and cleaned them on his handkerchief. Willow sorted her highlighters by color, then started a new folder on her laptop: “TASK FORCE.” Xander tapped his watch—the LED blinked once, then glowed steady green, in perfect sync with the Navy server.

Buffy folded her signed copy once, tucked it in her jacket, then looked around the table.

“So that’s it? We’re official?” Willow asked.

Buffy shrugged, not unkind. “We were always official. Now there’s paperwork.”

Outside, the last of the afternoon sun cut a thin gold line through the blinds, then vanished as the world slipped to night. Inside, the air was calm, the signatures drying, the tick of the clock louder than anything waiting in the dark.

They sat a while longer, not in a hurry to leave.

Scene 5

The first version of the integrated array occupied three library tables, two rolling carts, and enough extension cord to violate several fire codes at once. None of its pieces were new. That was the point. Willow’s perimeter monitor had been collecting the town’s fluctuations since the first anchor break. Ethan’s sigil logs gave those fluctuations a grammar. The portable ward rods from the SGC shipment could impose a known pattern, and the rugged sensor core Carter and Jenny had designed could tell them what the Hellmouth did in response.

What had not existed before was a way to make the pieces speak to one another.

Willow sat in the center of the sprawl with her laptop balanced on a milk crate, three adapter cables disappearing beneath the table. The program on her screen had begun as a timestamp matcher for Ethan’s meter readings. Over a month of late nights it had become a ward-resonance parser: part signal analysis, part municipal map, part translation layer for phenomena no computer manual admitted existed.

“The code can follow a repeating frequency,” she said, dragging a green trace over an amber one. “But magic doesn’t stay on a frequency just because we ask nicely. Every time the wards react, the signal shifts. Sometimes by a fraction, sometimes by a whole harmonic.”

Ethan leaned over her shoulder, his left hand holding the battered occult meter against the laptop’s plastic case. “It is not changing at random. It is answering. Push here, it moves there. Threaten the boundary, it gathers at the nearest lawful anchor.”

Giles stood at the far table with the municipal map under a transparent grid. He had marked the school, Restfield, City Hall, and the old utility lines in different colors, then connected them to the ward rods arranged around the room. “Then we do not need the machine to understand magic,” he said. “It needs to recognize the shape of the reply.”

Jenny set one of the sensor modules beside Willow. She had been back from Antarctica for six hours and had spent four of them replacing the module’s cold-weather shielding with a mesh ground that would not turn every active ward into static. “Carter’s array reads field pressure, phase, and energy density. Ethan gives us the expected relationships. Willow’s program compares the two in real time. If the pattern moves, we move with it.”

Buffy, sitting backward on a chair, said, “So it’s a magic radar.”

Four people objected at once.

“Magic radar,” Buffy repeated, satisfied.

The Navy communications specialist on the ladder pretended not to hear. He fed a cable through the open transom and up to the school roof, where four directional antenna loops had been fixed to the corners of the east wing. They were ordinary copper inside weatherproof housings, tuned low enough to catch the sensor modules without broadcasting the data beyond the local relay. A fifth loop, mounted on the library roof, served as the reference point.

O’Neil checked each installation against the diagram. “Passive receive unless Summers authorizes active mode. The data stays local. The Navy gets a copy after the event, not a live window into the school.”

Willow looked up. “And nobody remote can turn the transmitters on.”

“Physical switch only,” the specialist confirmed. He held up a red safety cover. “Your console, your hand.”

That earned him the first unqualified smile of the evening.

They tested the system with a ward Giles knew well enough to fail safely. He planted the white-oak stave at the center of a chalk-and-salt circle while Ethan opened a hairline seam at the western edge. The roof antennas registered the pressure change first. Carter’s sensor core translated it into a blue rise on Willow’s screen; half a second later, Ethan’s meter twitched and the chalk line brightened.

“Three sources, same event,” Willow said. She tagged the peak. “That’s our baseline.”

Ethan shifted the seam clockwise. The signal slid across the antenna grid, one node handing it to the next. Willow’s parser chased the movement, adjusting the comparison frequency in small steps until the traces overlapped again.

“Frequency chase,” she said. “It can stay with a moving pattern.”

Jenny watched the power draw climb. “Until the pattern starts feeding on the chase. If someone builds a recursive working, continuous correction becomes an amplifier.”

Ethan glanced at her. “An antenna instead of a lock.”

“Exactly.” Jenny reached past Willow and added a second control beneath the first. “We need a pulse mode. One sharp interruption, then silence before the working can use our output as a carrier.”

Willow named it SPIKE and gave the button a confirmation window. “Frequency chase for tracking. Spike mode for breaking a feedback loop. We do not press the exciting button unless somebody says the words ‘positive feedback.’”

“Or unless the sky opens,” Xander said.

“If the sky opens,” Giles replied, “we shall endeavor to be more specific.”

The portable command station took shape around the software. A folding camp table held Willow’s laptop and the sensor interface. Ethan drew an interlocked ward array directly onto its metal surface, each loop corresponding to one antenna node and each break giving him a place to interrupt the geometry by hand. Jenny marked the safe contact points in Starfleet blue and stocked her field kit with insulating patches, solvent, conductive markers, burn dressings, and the neon restraints Urgayle favored because nobody could pretend not to see them.

They ran the drill three times.

Giles held the axis and controlled the main ward. Willow stayed inside the defensive perimeter and called the frequencies. Ethan worked the hostile logic from the edge, where he could read or break a secondary pattern without crossing the primary line. Jenny moved between diagnostics and casualties. Buffy and Xander stayed mobile, one on the ground and one above it. The Navy watched the streets and did not enter the magical perimeter unless called.

On the first run, Willow chased the signal too long and the practice ward threw a spark through the laptop’s power brick. On the second, Ethan crossed behind Jenny without warning and earned an elbow to the ribs. On the third, the parser found the moving peak, Giles held it in place, Willow fired a controlled spike, and every light on the table went from amber to blue.

O’Neil stopped the clock. “Twenty-seven seconds. Again tomorrow.”

Buffy looked at the cables, the chalk, the roof feed, and the people standing in their assigned places. “Still calling it magic radar.”

This time nobody bothered to argue.

They packed the command table beneath the library steps with the cables already tied in sequence. The antenna grid stayed live in passive mode, listening to the ordinary pressure of the Hellmouth and teaching Willow’s parser what quiet looked like. By midnight, the blue baseline had become almost boring.

It stayed that way for three nights.

---

The allied readiness call ended with no one pretending the situation was under control. Britain committed airlift, Australia tracking corridors, France medical support, and China and Russia observer access with every legal objection preserved.

The exercise ran without Superman. Simulated orbital strikes took out two command nodes, but evacuation held because the response did not depend on one person crossing the sky. Only after the baseline held did Carter add Xander as an optional branch.

That was the difference between a miracle and a capability. Miracles were welcome. Capabilities got scheduled.

---



Chapter 35: The Sorcerer's End



Scene 1

The first warning was the taste, not the sound—the metallic spike that sliced the tongue, salt and ozone and cold copper, like biting down on a battery. The second warning was the shift in the air pressure, a sudden flattening of every surface, a pressurization that made the concrete crackle beneath Giles’s shoes.

It was 03:42 by the digital clock on Willow’s laptop and about ten seconds past the point where the sensible move was to call for Navy air support. Instead, the team deployed just as they’d trained: Giles at the axis, Willow and her gear inside the defensive perimeter, Ethan working the hostile’s logic from the edge, and Xander inbound from above, because there was no better way to punch through the new world’s layers than to fall on them.

The Hellmouth’s surface access point was a sheet of crumbling blacktop, backlot of the school, where generations of bored students had skidded bikes, cigarettes, and firecrackers under the rusted eye of the east wing security camera. Above it, the antenna loops on the east wing flashed from passive blue to warning amber. Tonight, the lot was webbed with fissures—thin lines of blue-white light tracing every stress fracture, pulsing to a rhythm that didn’t match any clock on earth. In the center, a triangle of standing figures: three sorcerers in cheap Windbreakers, faces streaked with sweat and war paint, chanting in Latin that alternated between midwestern and old-empire Vatican. At their feet, the glow of something worse.

Giles planted himself at the heart of a chalk-and-salt circle, bracing a stave of white oak against the ground, arms sleeved in residue from six different types of banishing powder. His glasses were off, tucked into his pocket, and his eyes locked on the geometry unfolding beneath him. “Keep the west perimeter clear,” he snapped, not bothering to check who was listening. “They’ll swing wide once the binding sets. Willow: update.”

Willow crouched over her makeshift command post—a folding camp table leveled with a folded library copy of Pride and Prejudice. Her hands hovered between her MacBook and a hand-drawn array of interlocked sigils, chalked directly onto the metal tabletop. The laptop’s screen scrolled telemetry from the resonance program she’d cobbled together over a month of late-night tests: it mapped the ward frequencies against the Hellmouth’s raw EM output, pinging red when they were in sync, blue when they were losing ground. Right now, everything was somewhere between magenta and ultraviolet, and the lines of the array vibrated hard enough to blur the edges. “They’re at sixty Hertz and climbing,” she called. “They’ve built a positive feedback loop—every watt we throw at the circle, they bounce back doubled. If we try to break the triangle head-on—”

“They’ll have a free channel to the core,” Giles finished, gaze never wavering from the line of sorcerers. “We hold. Ethan?”

Ethan stalked the lot’s far edge, jacket off and sleeves rolled, running a fingertip along the boundary he and Giles had spent weeks learning to read. His voice was dry but alert, a running monologue pitched just loud enough for the team but not the enemy. “They’re not punching through, Rupert. They’re tuning—adjusting resonance, as Willow said. But it’s not inward, it’s…” He stopped, ducked to trace a fresh sigil with a stick of chalk, then rose with a half-laugh. “Like a radio antenna, pointed up and out. Not a breach. A broadcast.”

Jenny stood to Giles’s left, her hair pinned in a no-nonsense twist, Starfleet-style field med kit slung across her shoulder and the other arm full of legal pads and Starfleet medical ward diagrams. She was the only one not visibly sweating; her composure was surgical. She called, “The central point is the anchor. The two outside are feeding. If you break the anchor, the whole thing will collapse. But only if you can do it in under thirty seconds. Otherwise it defaults to the backup array and starts over.”

Willow muttered, “That’s how I’d build it,” and flicked the trackpad, adjusting the resonance disruptor from frequency chase to spike mode. “If we break their buffer, it’ll destabilize the next two pulses. Maybe even cancel out the recursion.”

“Not maybe,” Giles said. “It will.” He angled the stave and drew a fresh set of runes around the western arc. “On my mark—”

Before he could finish, a shockwave hammered the lot, a zero-decibel concussive blast that drove the breath from every chest in a fifty-meter radius. The anchor sorcerer threw his head back, arms out, and the triangle’s apex lit up like a substation transformer in a storm.

Xander hit the ground at a run, feet landing hard enough to crater the asphalt, and arrowed for the nearest sorcerer. The ripple in the ritual field was instant: the chanting stuttered, the triangle flexed, and the nearest fissure spat up a plume of blue flame, hot enough to blacken the toes of Xander’s sneaker. He didn’t slow, but three steps in, the perimeter hit him like a car—every muscle in his body clenched, his jaw snapped shut, and his head rocked backward with the violence of a taser on full. He dropped to a knee, bounced back upright, and bared his teeth at the central point. “Barrier’s hard,” he called, the words half-muffled by the way his tongue refused to cooperate. “Like cellophane and a brick wall had a kid.”

Giles, eyes still on the ward, barked, “Not that way. Find the anchors—physical, not energy. Chalk lines, rods, candles, anything outside the perimeter. Disrupt them.”

Xander’s face went through a slow-motion cascade: anger, then calculation, then a flat acceptance. “Copy.” He spun on his heel and scanned the lot, looking for ritual hardware.

Willow, not pausing, snapped at Giles: “I can give him a microburst at the base frequency. Ten seconds, tops. Should scramble the outer points, but it’ll fry my array if it feeds back.”

“Do it,” Giles said. “We’ll patch your gear later.”

She nodded, toggled the macro, and the air over the table shimmered like a heat mirage, lines of chalk pulsing in time with her keystrokes. A low, digital whine cut through the chanting—Ethan grinned, “Nice—very Bletchley Park, Willow,”—and the two outer sorcerers staggered, hands flying to their ears. The triangle’s shape wobbled, then held.

Ethan, never one for missed drama, hopped the parking lot’s curb and closed the distance to the nearest fallen point. “They’ll have a physical backup. Most likely a secondary pattern, pre-scribed on the tarmac.” He crouched, then called, “Rupert, they’ve etched this one deep—military-grade scribing tool. Do you have solvent?”

Jenny, already moving, pulled a squeeze bottle from her med kit and lobbed it underhand; Ethan caught it without looking, then drenched the chalk line. Where the liquid touched, the white flared orange, then melted to sludge. The blue glow flickered, weakened.

In the center, the anchor sorcerer redoubled his chant, voice breaking into a hoarse shriek as he tried to restabilize the pattern. Giles recited his own countermeasures, voice low and fast: “Dei gratia, lux ex tenebris, fiat resolutio—” and pressed the stave to the line. The circle flashed, and for one breath the entire field was transparent: the chalk, the sigils, the veins of living blue energy, all naked and exposed.

Willow hit “EXECUTE” on her macro, and her array sang out—a pure, bell-like note that made every bone vibrate in sympathy. The fissures along the blacktop spasmed, then cinched shut, drawing the energy back toward the triangle. Xander, running flat out, found the first physical anchor: a short rebar spike at the parking lot’s north corner, wrapped in waxed thread and marked with runes he half-recognized from a dead language. He yanked it loose, concrete splintering, then tossed it into the nearest trash can.

Giles shouted, “Next one—northwest!” and Xander was moving before the echo died.

Ethan’s hands, up to the knuckles in greasy blue residue, worked a second anchor, scraping at it with the edge of a car key. Jenny was at his side, a bandage already unrolled, wrapping his wrist in a practiced one-handed motion. “You keep ruining your hands,” she said, voice clinical but with a steel thread. “I’ll run out of tape before morning.”

Ethan said, “I like your tape,” and then “Got it,” as the last of the secondary sigil came away under the key’s pressure.

Willow’s macro started to glitch—her own hand flickered between keys as the feedback loop surged, lines of code and chalk turning the color of TV static. “It’s peaking. Giles, I need to—”

“Release,” Giles said, and she did. The energy bucked, then collapsed, the triangle snapping shut as if someone had popped the world’s meanest balloon.

The anchor sorcerer went to his knees, hands to his face, then slumped forward in the dirt.

For a half second, nothing happened.

Then, from the hairline crack at the center of the lot, a new pulse erupted: not blue, not white, but a bruised, molten red, and with it a voice—raw, desperate, too many years of nicotine and bad coffee behind it—howling, “NOW!”

The third sorcerer, the one who had barely moved, tossed a canister through the closing triangle, where it landed at Giles’s feet.

Giles tried to kick it, but the canister burst, spraying the inside of the ward circle with an oily mist. His vision doubled, then tripled, every color blooming at the edges. He dropped to one knee, stave digging a hole in the blacktop.

Jenny, moving faster than reflex, stepped in front of Giles, Starfleet patch in hand, and pressed the sticker to his temple. “Breathe,” she said, and Giles did, the world returning in a rush.

Ethan tackled the third sorcerer, rolled him flat, and zip-tied his wrists with three fast loops of neon plastic. “Stay down,” Ethan said, in a voice that left no room for discussion.

Willow looked up from her table, face flushed with static, eyes wild. “The array is… still running,” she said, and pointed. “There’s a secondary spike, higher frequency. They left it on a timer.”

Giles, eyes clearing, called, “Willow, counterpulse on my mark.” He angled the stave again, braced his feet, and watched as the red energy boiled up from the fissure, aiming not for the team but for the sky. “Now!”

Willow toggled the disruptor, and the whole parking lot hummed, every surface vibrating in sympathy. The red pulse shuddered, lost shape, and then bled off into nothing, leaving only the stench of burnt ozone and the hiss of the sprinkler system, which had come on early thanks to the spike in soil temperature.

Silence. Then the low, nasal whine of an ambulance, somewhere beyond the fence.

Xander walked to the center of the lot, eyes on the still-twitching anchor sorcerer, then at Giles. “You good?”

“Better than him,” Giles said, nodding at the sorcerer. “Go help Ethan and Jenny with the others.”

Xander went. Giles stood, steadying himself, and looked around the battlefield: Willow’s array was scorched, the folding table blackened at one edge; the triangle’s outer points were empty, except for the fading afterimages of the failed ritual; Jenny already had her med kit open, triaging Ethan’s hands and the unconscious sorcerers with equal focus.

Giles put his glasses back on and took stock: all accounted for, all upright, all in one piece.

He let himself exhale.

But behind the glasses, his eyes never left the last red flicker in the crack.

They’d won the first round, but something beneath the lot was still listening.

Scene 2

Giles was already halfway through the next round of defensive runes when Willow’s voice cut through the static. “I’m in. I can feed the disruptor now, but the harmonics are spiking off chart—anything not shielded is going to fry.”

Giles never stopped moving, but managed, “You have full discretion, Willow.”

“Copy that,” she said, fingers flickering over the keyboard, the screen awash in shifting waveform overlays. The sigil array on her table—every line laid by her own hand, her own precise sense of symmetry—began to strobe with blue-white light, each pulse matched to the microsecond by the line on her screen. At the first volley, the nearest fissure on the asphalt stuttered, its glow guttering, then rebounding with a snap that sent a jolt up her arm. She gritted her teeth and upped the gain.

At the center, Giles advanced the ward by a meter, the new circle overlaying the old, layered with salt, chalk, and three drops of blood from the ritual cut on his left thumb. The ground shuddered in protest, but Giles’s voice never wavered, even as a hairline crack in the concrete chased the circle’s edge. “Ethan, now.”

Ethan, already at a dead sprint, dove for the triangle’s southern point, dropped to a knee, and, with no hesitation, scraped his thumbnail across the anchor sorcerer’s chalk line. The circle here was not drawn but carved—fresh, bright white against the battered blacktop, with precision that spoke of both practice and intent. As his nail broke the line, the chanting at this point skipped, a double-clutch as the ritual’s logic lost a step.

Ethan’s grin was savage. “That’s the trick, isn’t it—if you can’t match the energy, cheat the geometry.” He took a fresh piece of chalk and jammed it into the break, disrupting the line’s flow with a deliberate, ugly squiggle. The sorcerer at this point staggered, clutching at the air as if the ritual itself was now a physical burden.

The anchor, at the triangle’s apex, was a scarecrow of a man, fortyish, his face shadowed under a truck-stop baseball cap, Wolfram & Hart’s badge clipped ostentatiously to his belt as if the company ID alone could ward off the world’s monsters. He broke from the chant to shout a command—unintelligible but charged with the authority of someone who had paid for this power in decades of debts. The two subordinates faltered, their voices overlapping in a discordant spiral, and the nexus construct at the triangle’s center began to pulse in sick, uneven beats.

Jenny sprinted to Ethan’s side, medical kit slapping at her hip, and dropped to a crouch. “Don’t touch the line again with skin,” she said, her own two fingers landing a millimeter from the break. From a vest pocket she produced a slim, Starfleet-blue marker and, with one precise stroke, drew a tiny sigil next to the gap: a caduceus twisted into a Möbius, the badge of her own mastery. The chalk at the southern point went dull, then black. The sorcerer collapsed, gasping, but alive.

Giles, sensing the break, intoned a new verse—his Latin bone-dry and academic but saturated with the kind of force that made small children and dogs shiver in sympathy. The ward circle flexed, then with a lurch snapped out another meter, nearly brushing the edge of the triangle. The ground heaved again, but Giles rode the motion, steady as a pendulum.

Xander, out at the lot’s farthest corner, found the first iron stake, embedded flush with the concrete. He gripped it with both hands and pulled—a simple act, but the metal fought him, as if magnetized to the bedrock. Muscles bunched in his arms, sweat popping from his forehead in neat rows, and the stake gave with a sound like a snapped femur, the runes on it sizzling out in a brief flare of blue. Xander took three steps to the next anchor—repeat, snap, burn—and the perimeter’s tension dropped by half, a pressure released so quickly that Willow’s table almost vibrated off its plastic feet.

“Two left,” Willow muttered, feeding another spike of harmonics into the array. The pulses now came so fast they blurred, and the chalk lines on the table started to melt at the edges, the air above hot enough to boil sweat from her palms.

Ethan, his hands stinging from contact with the ritual energy, started sidestepping to the next sorcerer, but Jenny called, “Leave him—if you break the last stake, the southern point will fail on its own.” She watched, eyes bright and clinical, then radioed, “Xander, the east lot. Final anchor’s in the drainage grate.”

“On it,” he said, already moving, his legs barely skidding across the crumbling surface. At the edge of the grate, the third iron stake gleamed in the half-light, runes still pulsing weakly. He yanked it free and tossed it onto the blacktop, where it rolled to a stop and shed its last shimmer like smoke.

In the center of the triangle, the fissures went dead, their blue glow winking out in a rolling wave. The chanting collapsed into coughing, the two remaining sorcerers dropping to the ground, limp and spent.

For one glorious second, it looked like victory.

Then the ground at the triangle’s center split wide, the concrete popping like a blister, and a new construct—this one not an array but a ragged, unstable vortex—tore open two meters above the surface. Its edges burned the same blue-white as Willow’s chalk array, but inside was only blackness and wind, roaring and hungry. The anchor sorcerer, alone now, clawed his way upright and, in three desperate steps, flung himself through the portal, a battered leather satchel clutched to his chest.

The portal snapped shut, leaving only the thud of his shoes echoing against the far side.

The rest was aftermath.

Willow slammed her hands down on the table. “He took the resonance map.” Her voice was quiet, flat. “The whole frequency profile of the ward network—he has it. They can target every gap, every weak node. They’ll know the timing of the next three resets before we do.”

Jenny pulled her hands back from the cooling chalk and started taping Ethan’s bruised fingers, even as she stared at the black spot where the portal had been. “Did you see it?” she asked, not to anyone in particular. “That was not just a teleport. That was a handoff. They were waiting for him on the other side.”

Giles, breathing hard, let the stave fall to the ground, then knelt to examine the scorch mark left by the portal. He reached out, careful, and drew a circle around the residue, then checked his own fingers for burns. “It’s a double-layer construct,” he said, almost to himself. “First layer disables our countermeasures, second allows exfiltration. I’ve never seen it pulled off this cleanly before.”

Willow, eyes on her screen, said, “The Hellmouth’s hum is up by thirty percent. There’s an irregularity now—two overlapping pulses. Ours, and theirs. They’ll use that interference to hide the next move.”

Ethan, flexing his rewrapped hand, said, “That’s the point, isn’t it. If you know your enemy’s sensors, you can walk the line all night. We’re in for another wave, Rupert.”

Giles stood, every joint in his body arguing with the motion, and faced the team. “Not tonight. They’ll need hours to recalibrate. We hold the ground for now, and patch the network before sunrise. Willow, is your backup on an isolated drive?”

She nodded. “Only on this machine, and the one in the library safe.”

“Destroy the drive. Move the safe.” Giles’s eyes swept the ruined lot, the groaning of the earth now a dull but constant undertone. “They can’t use what they don’t have.”

Willow hesitated, then powered down the laptop, the screen going black with a final, reluctant chime.

The two captured sorcerers groaned against the wall, hands zip-tied behind their backs, the sweat drying on their faces in the cool night air. Xander, still breathing hard, walked to the edge of the lot and stared at the spot where the anchor had vanished, fists unclenching by slow degrees.

The ground shuddered, once, then stilled.

Ethan looked at Giles, then at the others. “How bad is it, Rupert?”

Giles put his glasses on, blinking twice as if to clear his vision. “Bad. But not fatal.”

He looked at each of them in turn: Willow, red-eyed but upright; Jenny, fingers stained with three people’s blood, still taping bandages; Xander, hair plastered to his forehead, but standing straight.

“We lost the secret,” Giles said. “But we kept the world.”

He paused, then managed a smile.

“We’re still here.”

Beneath the ruined blacktop, the Hellmouth’s new song thrummed in a minor key, uneven and unpredictable—a warning, a wound, and a promise.

Scene 3

They worked the scene in three shifts: triage, assessment, and containment.

The two captured sorcerers were zip-tied wrist and ankle, propped against the cold brick of the school’s east wall, breathing in short, uneven gulps. Giles had checked their pockets for concealed charms and come away with a haul of salt canisters, two vials of powdered wolfsbane, and a pre-paid phone loaded with a single number in the contacts: “HOLCROFT.” The name meant nothing to him, but he logged it on the first page of the next clean notebook.

Jenny did triage with the focus of a field medic in a hot zone. She started on Ethan’s hands: both burned, right worse than left, the skin along the knuckles puckered from the reaction between the custom chalk and whatever the sorcerer had laced the lines with. Ethan made no complaint, just watched with clinical detachment as Jenny disinfected, applied burn gel, and wrapped his hands with a technique that would have impressed a hospital nurse.

He flexed his fingers, the blue of the burn ointment glowing faintly in the ambient light, and said, “Remind me next time not to use my own skin as a resistor, would you?”

Jenny smiled thinly, not breaking focus. “You always say that and you never remember.”

“I’ve had worse. Cantrip in Budapest, 1987. Whole hand turned chartreuse for a week.”

Jenny finished the wrap, checked his pulse, and then moved on to Willow, who hadn’t moved from her perch at the edge of the camp table. Willow’s forearm was stippled with tiny abrasions from the chalk-line blowback, one gash deep enough to need a butterfly bandage. She barely looked up as Jenny cleaned and patched it. “If I rekey the array at random, they’ll expect it. But if I phase-shift the node frequencies by less than point zero one, we can stay ahead of the curve for maybe… seven hours, tops. Giles’ll need to keep the circle live the whole time.”

Giles, crouched over the ritual’s remains with a penlight, didn’t bother to look up. “Consider it done. Xander, how’s the perimeter?”

Xander hadn’t said much since the portal closed. He patrolled the lot’s edge, boots crunching over the spent chalk and the ash from the failed ritual. “Clear. But I keep hearing the hum, Giles. It’s not just in the ground anymore.”

“That’s to be expected.” Giles pulled out his glasses, wiped them on his shirt, and made a careful study of the scribed geometry, copying it into his notebook with a speed born of years in academia and desperation alike. “They’ve altered the frequency. The resonance is… off. Not like last time.”

Willow finished a diagnostic run, the lines of code reflected in her irises. “They’re using the map already. I can see the network stress in real time. It’s not random—they’re probing for a systemic fault, not a local one. If they can push the right node, they can invert the whole thing. That’s… that’s not great, Giles.”

He nodded, pressing two fingers to the bridge of his nose. “Then we don’t let them. You’ll have to monitor the whole network from here.”

Willow: “I’ll need two hours to write a scrambling daemon, and another hour to harden it against counterattack. Three, if the nodes keep degrading.”

Giles: “You have two.”

Jenny finished with Willow, wiped her hands, and called, “Ethan, you’re on watch with Xander. I want you watching the physical perimeter as well as the magical one.”

Ethan’s smile was almost grateful. “Always best to guard the doors.”

They fell into a rhythm: Giles documenting every scrap of the failed array, Willow on her laptop, running permutations and muttering code to herself; Xander walking the lot, checking every shadow, every whisper in the air; Ethan leaning against the wall, hands bandaged, but eyes scanning the street for any sign of a second strike. Jenny cleaned up the med kit, organized the spent supplies, and took three minutes to do her own quick diagnostic on the sorcerers—vitals, pupils, and a search for hidden suicide capsules, of which she found two and quietly pocketed them for evidence.

Once, as the hour stretched, Giles saw Willow staring at the blacktop where the anchor sorcerer had vanished. Her face was set, but he recognized the look: the same one he’d seen on war reporters, the one that knew the next battle would be harder than the last.

He set a hand on her shoulder. “You did well tonight. We all did.”

She nodded, then said, “It’s not over.”

Giles smiled, small and sad. “No, it never is.”

They worked until dawn, the lot finally growing quiet, the only sound the faint, off-tempo hum from the ground, and the slow, steady tapping of Giles’s pen against the page.

When he closed the notebook, the two restrained sorcerers were slumped, exhausted, against the wall; Willow was asleep, head pillowed on her arms; Ethan and Xander were sitting on the trunk of a patrol car, talking low and laughing once in a while, the sound softer than you’d expect, but real. Jenny paced the perimeter, her shadow long in the morning light, med kit at her side.

It wasn’t victory, not yet. But it was survival.

Beneath them, the Hellmouth thrummed its new, unpredictable rhythm. But above ground, the world kept its shape—cracked, scorched, a little worn at the edges, but whole.

For now.



Chapter 36: What You Become



Scene 1

The rooftop had never been his, exactly, but it was one of the few places in Sunnydale that hadn’t changed even when the rest of the town had gotten wise to weirdness. The tar felt like an old basketball court under him—yielding just enough to register the weight, still warm from the day’s sun, never quite letting go of the heat. Xander Harris lay on his back, knees up, elbows splayed, staring at the squared geometry of the night sky as it was diced into blocks by the high perimeter lamps and the rising heads of the HVAC units. The parking lot below was a geometry lesson, empty except for a janitor’s truck with the company logo applied at an angle, as if the decal had been installed by someone with a vendetta against symmetry.

He rolled the watch over in his palm: matte black, government-issue, the band still creased where Carter had cinched it to fit his wrist, as if the only way the device could be safe was if it had first passed through the hands of someone who believed in cause and effect. Carter’s cause: safety. Her effect: the world not falling apart for one more day.

He flicked the band, catching the familiar snap as it unlatched. It was a good sound. Not quite as satisfying as biting into a day-old Krispy Kreme—the proper, glazed kind, no filling, no nonsense—but a close second. The doughnut was already half gone, the bag collapsed in a way that exposed the remaining half-moon to the night air. The rooftop did not judge. Xander took a bite, letting the icing melt to paste between his tongue and the roof of his mouth.

He set the watch down beside the bag. It looked at home there, in an orphan’s row of things people left behind when they decided they were above it all. The donut, the watch. Obligation and breakfast, sitting in the same patch of gravel. He weighed them: the donut would disappear soon enough, but the watch would keep pulsing, a heartbeat of someone else’s idea of responsibility.

He looked at them, side by side, and wondered which one would get him through tomorrow.

The watch was a one-way line: the kind that never asked what he wanted, only if he would answer when called. He let it rest, dial facing up, and finished the donut. Each chew was a small act of defiance. The kind you could get away with, if no one else was around.

A breeze came up from the north, laced with the smell of fertilizer from the practice field and, faintly, the sharp ozone of the transformer box by the gym. There was a hum to the air—HVAC units, a distant car alarm on Revello Drive, the mutter of strip-mall neon blocks away. From here, the school was both fortress and island: above the world but never out of its reach.

The gym lights bled yellow through the frosted glass of the skylights, pooling on the roof in diluted rectangles. Xander shifted until one fell across his face, then closed his eyes and tried to remember the last time he felt sun-warm and safe at the same time. He gave up after five seconds, opened his eyes, and watched the shape of his own hand cross the light as he reached for the watch.

He buckled it on. The weight was familiar now; he’d worn heavier things in shop class, or in those summers when you learned to swing a hammer before you learned to swing a punch. The band caught a patch of sweat on his wrist, the cold sticking to skin for an instant before the warmth took over. He twisted the dial once, then again, just to make sure it still worked.

Down below, the janitor’s truck started up. The engine was a cough, then a grumble. The headlamps swept the base of the building, catching the fire escape in full relief. Xander swung his legs over the edge, boots scraping the pebbled ledge, and tossed the empty doughnut bag at the air. It caught a draft, hovered a moment, then landed next to the rusted metal of the ladder. He considered flying down—why not, when you could—but the idea felt cheap, like skipping chapters just to get to the end. So he climbed, step by step, hands on the railings, boots ringing the rungs, the world returning to its proper size with every foot of descent.

At the bottom, he didn’t look back up.

The watch on his wrist was quiet, inert, for now. In his chest, a similar mechanism waited for its own signal. On Revello Drive, the car alarm finally cut out. The parking lot was empty.

He walked home.

Scene 2

Giles’s apartment was finally quiet. The research books had been closed, Ethan had gone upstairs, and Giles was in the kitchen making enough noise with the kettle to announce that he was deliberately not listening. Xander sat beside the secure telephone with the post-it note from his wallet, its corner curled where it had stuck to an old receipt. Zoey’s Georgetown number was written beneath the White House routing phrase she had given him months ago.

He entered the routing code and asked the White House operator to connect him.

The secure line clicked through two exchanges, then rang. A student answered: “Georgetown, Copley Hall, room 412.”

He waited. The sound of footsteps, and then Zoey’s voice—warm, clipped, with that perpetual undertone of not-quite-daring you to argue with her. “Hello?”

The operator said, “Secure call for Zoey Bartlet from Xander Harris.”

A beat, then Zoey said, “You know most people call their friends without routing through the White House. Or is this a California thing?”

He could hear her smiling. “Put him through.”

It clicked. “Hey,” he said.

“Hey yourself. So what’s the emergency?” she asked. “Alien landing? Principal Snyder declare martial law? Or did you just remember that my meal plan comes with unlimited minutes for emotional support?”

“No emergency,” Xander said. “Just wanted to check in. You busy?”

She said, “I was working on a twelve-page response paper on the roots of European integration, but the urge to procrastinate has never been stronger.” A pause, then, “Tell me something good.”

He looked at the dark window above Giles’s desk, at the reflection of the lamp and the old municipal map still spread beneath it. “There was a cat stuck in a tree on First and North. I didn’t get it down. But I watched, and a little kid climbed up and got it himself, and then his mom grounded him for being reckless, which I think defeats the whole point of cats.”

Zoey snorted. “I bet you could have gotten it down without even breaking the branch.”

He shrugged. “Maybe. But it looked like he wanted to do it himself.”

She shifted on her end. He could hear the pop of a soda can and a muted “dammit” as it fizzed over. “So what’s really up?” she asked. “Because I know you, Xander, and you don’t call me unless you’ve been thinking about something for at least two hours.”

He made a show of being wounded. “That’s so unfair. I have layers. I have—”

She cut in: “—the ability to sulk like an Olympic event, yes. Now talk.”

He let a breath out. “Is the credentials thing tomorrow supposed to be a real deal, or just a formality? Like, are they actually putting me in a database, or is it just so they can say I’m not an ‘anomaly’ anymore?”

“Both,” Zoey said, instantly. “Welcome to American government. Everything is about filing you, and also about making you feel like it was your idea.”

He made a noise. “So I’m just the guy they want to tag and bag, but with cake at the end.”

“I hope there’s cake,” Zoey said. “But here’s the part you should pay attention to: my dad is good at making people think the show is for them, but the White House is always the real audience. Except with you, I think it’s actually both. He gets weird about favor banks—he talks about it in staff meetings, and then Leo tries to turn it into a flowchart, and my dad hates that. He wants to owe you, not employ you.”

Xander squinted, trying to picture this. “He wants to what?”

“He wants to owe you,” she repeated. “You did something, you didn’t ask for anything, and now you’re in his head. Not as an asset, but as someone he actually owes. It’s very old-school Catholic. Or maybe it’s just how he was raised.”

He ran his thumb along the reinforced cord of the secure handset. “And how do you know all this?”

“Because I have ears, Xander. And because my dad talks like he’s the only one in the room when he’s annoyed.” She softened a little. “Also, because I pay attention.”

He let himself smile. “You’re scary, you know that?”

“Only if you’re in charge of the dining hall,” she said. “Oh! Did I tell you about the lasagna incident?”

He shook his head, even though she couldn’t see it. “No. But now I have to know.”

“So there’s a senator’s aide, like, first week on campus, and he’s trying to be impressive at the table, right? He tells everyone how real lasagna is made with béchamel and not ricotta, and then he stands up to make a big speech, and the tray slips, and he dumps the entire serving—cheese, sauce, everything—down the back of the Dean’s suit. The Dean, who has a standing bet with the President about which of their kids can go longest without a headline in the student newspaper. Guess who lost?”

He laughed, and the sound surprised him. It felt real, not just a social noise.

“Did you ever have a food fight in your cafeteria?” she asked.

He thought about the messes at Sunnydale High, and the one time he tried to stand up to the football team, and how that ended with him wearing a week’s worth of Jell-O. “Once. I lost. Badly.”

Zoey’s tone shifted again. “So why the nerves, Xander? Is it the watch?”

He looked down at his wrist. “You ever wear something and feel like it was going to own you after a while? Like, not the shoes you break in, but the kind you buy because you think it’ll change you, and then it just reminds you every time you look at it?”

“Shoes, no,” Zoey said. “But once, my dad bought me this necklace. It was supposed to be a shield, or a charm, or whatever. All it did was catch on everything, and eventually I just stopped wearing it. When he noticed, he said, ‘I thought it would make you feel protected.’ I told him it made me feel like a kid who needed protection. Which is not the same thing.”

He spun the watch dial, slow. “If I throw this away, Carter would probably kill me.”

“Don’t throw it away. Upgrade it. Or draw something on it,” she said. “You’re allowed to change the things they give you, you know. The real power is when you make it yours.”

He chewed on that, then said, “You think the new set will be better?”

She said, “I think if they’re making both receivers two-way, it means they’re finally interested in what you have to say back. Which is the opposite of owning you.”

He held the phone, then said, “Will you be watching? For the ceremony?”

Zoey gave a warm little snort. “You think I’d miss it? I’ll have a notebook out and everything. If you do anything embarrassing, it’s going in the next call home.”

He nodded. “Thanks, Zoey.”

She lowered her voice, serious now. “Promise me you’ll call after. You can even route it through the White House again if you enjoy terrifying the duty operator.”

He said, “I promise.”

The line went quiet except for the kettle settling in the kitchen and the faint hum of the secure-line equipment beneath Giles’s desk.

He hung up and sat for a moment with the post-it in his hand, the ink beginning to blur where his thumb pressed too hard. Then he folded it twice, tucked it back into his wallet, and reached for his jacket.

The night was soft, the world a little less sharp-edged than before. He started walking home, the watch heavy on his wrist, but not as heavy as the promise he’d just made.

Scene 3

The headstones had been knocked down during a vampire nest cleanout three nights back, when the usual suspects—Buffy, Giles, and the new Navy guy with the tactical goatee—decided to take the direct approach and hit the crypt after dark. The vamps got dusted, the crypt got repainted in plasma, and a row of granite markers got torpedoed in the crossfire. Xander had watched from the tree line, not because he wasn’t invited, but because the last three times he’d tried to help, he’d caught the blunt end of someone else’s heroics and spent the night picking marble shards out of his hair.

The cemetery wasn’t quiet, exactly, but it was the kind of quiet you could work in. Xander gripped the edge of a fallen headstone with both hands and leaned his weight into it, rolling the rectangle upright until it thunked back onto its base. The engraved dates said “1922 – 1968,” but the name itself—“Fennimore”—was the only part still readable. He pressed the stone, tested its balance, then moved to the next one.

Buffy dropped onto the lid of a nearby crypt, knees drawn up, chin propped on one. “If you keep going, you’re going to alphabetize them.”

He shrugged. “I’m more of a Dewey Decimal guy. But I’ll settle for less corpse chaos.”

She watched him for a long beat. “So. This is your training now?”

He dusted his hands off, not looking at her. “It’s cardio. And less likely to get me an exorcism bill from the city.”

She slid down, boots hitting the grass with a thump, and circled around until she was in his line of sight. “You want to spar, or just talk in metaphors all day?”

He hesitated. Then: “If I say metaphors, do I still have to do the pushups?”

“No, but I’ll make fun of you for being emotionally avoidant,” she said. She squared off, but left her hands at her sides.

He took a ready stance. Not perfect, but better than a year ago. She didn’t wait for a signal—she just closed the distance and threw a three-count: jab, cross, knee. He blocked the first, ate the second, and stumbled back as her knee came up, stopping a centimeter short of his ribs.

She lowered her leg. “You’re distracted,” she said. “Which is not good. That’s how you lose the other kidney.”

He grinned, a real one. “I was just giving you the win. It’s your cemetery, after all.”

She let that ride. “You ever think about what comes after all this?” she asked, eyes narrowing. “Like, after high school, after Sunnydale, after the weird stops being weird?”

He tried to picture it, failed, and shrugged. “Maybe. If I live that long.”

She stepped back, then circled, making him turn to keep her in view. “There’s a difference between having power and using it,” she said. “That’s what Giles is always on about, but he never explains how you’re supposed to know which one you are.”

He waited. She was working up to something.

“You know what I figured out, running the briefings with Chegwidden?” she said. “The soldiers who freeze aren’t the ones who don’t know what to do. They’re the ones who haven’t decided who they are, when things go wrong. The rest just act.”

He snorted. “So what, I need a mantra? A secret identity?”

Buffy stopped, hands on hips. “No. I’m saying you need to stop letting everyone else write the narrative. You get to choose, Xander.”

He started to argue, but she cut him off with a look. “Who are you when things go wrong?”

He thought about it, about the last night on the roof, the phone call, the taste of metal and the heaviness of the watch.

He said, “The guy who shows up.”

She shook her head. “That’s not an answer. That’s a habit.”

She stepped in, quick, and threw a lazy elbow—telegraphed, easy to dodge. He stepped aside this time, not blocking, just letting the force slide past him. It was a different move, and she noticed.

She smiled, a little proud. “See? You’re learning.”

He looked at her, sweat starting to bead at his hairline, the grass at his feet pressed flat from his constant shifting. “Is this the part where you tell me to keep my head down and just survive high school?”

She shook her head. “Nope. I’m telling you that the ceremony tomorrow isn’t a leash, it’s a door. You walk through, or you hover outside and keep being weird. Your call.”

He stared at her, then down at his hands. The knuckles were already bruising from the granite, but he didn’t mind. He picked up the last headstone—a small one, “HARRIS” in block letters, no dates—and set it back on its marker.

Buffy watched him do it. “I never asked you to be part of the team,” she said, “but I’m glad you keep showing up anyway.”

He straightened the stone, brushed the dirt from its face, and nodded. “It’s what I do.”

She gave a crooked little smile, then jogged off, her feet barely leaving a trace on the grass.

Xander stood in the afternoon sun, the silence settling in again, and wondered which part of himself he’d left behind on the cemetery floor.

Scene 4

The bookshop was one of the old ones, the kind that sold more mass than margin: a narrow storefront, chipped paint, the scent of dry rot and accidental incense. Inside, it was books to the ceiling—some actual, some not. Giles had the back room on a retainer, cash only, and in exchange the owner turned a blind eye to the strange hours and the even stranger company.

The training space was all atmosphere: a battered worktable in the center, its surface pocked with burns and ink blots; a single metal chair, bent at the legs; floor-to-ceiling shelves of occult texts, most with titles in Latin or Greek, none with dust jackets. The only light was a naked bulb dangling from a cord, its pull chain long enough to trip on if you weren’t careful.

Ethan had the latest lesson laid out before Xander arrived: a small brass bowl filled with sand the color of old bone, the next exercise in the same sequence they had been working through for weeks. He sat with his legs crossed at the ankle, rolling a thin cigarette between two fingers. Without looking up, he flicked it into a second bowl already half-full of spent cigarettes and gestured for Xander to take his usual chair.

“Punctuality,” Ethan said, voice flat. “One of the few virtues Giles managed to cultivate. I thought you might rebel.”

Xander sat. The chair protested, a metallic squeal. “I’m trying to work on self-improvement.”

Ethan’s lips twitched at that. He reached out, index finger extended, and drew a perfect circle in the sand. “Look,” he said.

Xander looked. “Circle.”

“Unbroken,” Ethan clarified. He wiped it away and drew a second, identical except for a single line bisecting it at a shallow angle.

“Now,” Ethan said, “which is stronger?”

Xander stared. “The one with the line. It’s got a support.”

“Wrong,” Ethan said, not gloating, just correcting. “The line is a seam. That’s where you break it.” He pressed his thumb into the center of the bisected circle. The sand collapsed along the division, splitting clean. He did the same to the whole circle: the thumbprint deformed the shape, but it stayed one piece.

Xander nodded, absorbing. “So… seams are bad.”

Ethan shrugged. “Seams are inevitable. But they’re also predictable. That’s why every spell, every curse, every compulsion Giles ever taught you to resist, starts with an attack on the seam. On the place where you’re not one thing or the other.”

He wiped the sand flat, drew both circles again. “You’ve got two seams now, Xander. The one that runs down the middle of your name, and the one that runs down the middle of your new job.”

Xander tapped the edge of the bowl. “And if I just… don’t care about the seam?”

Ethan’s smile was dry. “Then you’re either a Buddha, or a corpse. Most people never get there.”

He paused, as if deciding whether to waste a breath on philosophy. “If you’re going to survive—really survive, not just keep getting up—you need to stop thinking of yourself as two things. There’s no Alexander Harris and Superman. There’s just you. And the world will push until you pick one, or until the seam splits.”

He waited, like he expected Xander to argue. When Xander didn’t, he nodded, as if that was the only correct answer.

Xander looked at the circles, at the line of collapse. “So, this is a magic thing? Or a life thing?”

Ethan snorted, then leaned back. “Magic, life, same difference. The universe doesn’t care about the category—just about the pressure.”

Xander stared at the sand for a long second, then drew his own finger through both circles, erasing them with a single, side-to-side sweep.

He said, “Show me the next exercise.”

Ethan reached for another bowl, this one shallower, and pulled it in front of him with care. “This is for mapping,” he said. “But the principle’s the same.”

He poured out a thin layer of blue powder, then traced an oval, not a circle, in the powder’s surface. “The shape can change,” he said. “But the integrity matters more.”

Xander watched the bowl, watched Ethan’s finger cut through the shape, watched the way the powder held its new edge even after the finger was gone.

Ethan nodded at him, serious now. “Try it.”

Xander reached out, hesitated, then drew a quick line through the blue, dividing it in half. The edge split, clean but obvious. He pressed his finger to the middle, just like before, and the two halves slid away from each other, no resistance.

Ethan watched, not smiling. “This is what the Council never teaches,” he said. “If you want to hold under pressure, you have to want to be whole.”

He leaned in, voice a little softer. “Otherwise you’ll spend your life patching seams, and the world will keep finding the weak spot. Every. Single. Time.”

Xander thought about it, then wiped the surface clean again, flattening the blue powder until it was almost perfect.

He looked at Ethan. “What if you don’t know what whole looks like?”

Ethan shrugged, but it wasn’t dismissive. “Nobody does. You fake it until you don’t have to anymore. The trick is, don’t fake it for the world—just for yourself.”

He rolled another cigarette, fingers quick and precise. “Let’s try the next one,” he said, voice sliding back to business.

Xander nodded, ready.

Scene 5

The conference room in the Sunnydale Naval Reserve facility was the kind of room that inspired lowercase letters—unremarkable, wood-paneled, lit by a bank of fluorescent tubes that made everything look a shade less trustworthy. There was a flag in the corner, a credenza along the far wall with a coffee pot and four mismatched mugs, and a long table with just enough veneer left to reflect the ceiling’s light back at you. Someone had thought to install a high-definition camera on the end of the table, angled to catch the whole room and whatever dignity could be salvaged by the arrangement of the chairs.

Xander took a seat. The metal frame creaked, old and Navy-issue. He tugged at his shirt collar, which had decided to shrink by half a size since his last growth spurt.

Captain Carter entered three minutes later, carrying a manila folder, two fountain pens, and what looked like a small jewelry case. She was in civilian clothes: a navy blue blazer, white shirt open at the throat, pants a touch too formal for “just passing through.” Xander noticed, and for the first time that day, he felt a little less like a specimen.

“Ready?” Carter asked. She set the folder on the table and sat one seat over, the better to avoid crowding him.

“About as ready as I get,” Xander said. He tried to sound cool, but the echo of the HVAC system made every syllable ring out.

Carter smiled, tight but genuine. “I know it’s weird. Just pretend you’re in the principal’s office, but the principal is the President.”

“That’s not weird at all,” he deadpanned.

She reached over and toggled the remote on the table. The far end of the room came alive: a wall-mounted screen flickered, then stabilized on a video call interface. The White House crest, a cheap digital watermark, hovered in the lower right corner.

Bartlet was already there, at his desk, a stack of blue folders at his left and a mug (presidential seal, possibly collector’s edition) at his right. Leo McGarry stood in the background, arms folded, gaze somewhere between protective and bored.
